These percentages serve as an internal educational and disciplinary model for evaluating a practitioner\'s commitment to renunciation, readiness for ordination, and continued suitability for monastic life. They are not measurements of spiritual attainment, nor do they replace the formal requirements of Pabbajjā, Upasampadā, or the Vinaya.

**5.1. The Progressive Levels of Renunciation**

Renunciation develops progressively through ethical discipline, the relinquishment of worldly pleasures, the reduction of worldly responsibilities, and the sustained dedication of one\'s life to spiritual cultivation.

The Order ordinarily recognizes the following levels as reference points in this progression:

- **0% --- Ethical Lay Practitioner:** A layperson who observes the Five Precepts and lives ethically while continuing to participate fully in household life, employment, family responsibilities, social relationships, and ordinary worldly activities. Ethical conduct is the foundation of spiritual development, but it does not in itself constitute a substantial relinquishment of the household way of life.

- **20% --- Uposatha Practitioner:** An ethical lay practitioner who regularly undertakes the Eight Precepts on **Uposatha** days (or roughly once a week), including celibacy and the renunciation of certain ordinary pleasures. The practitioner begins to experience a more deliberate separation from household enjoyments and develops familiarity with the discipline of renunciation.

- **40% --- Upāsaka / Upāsikā in Dedicated Training:** A practitioner who has made a sustained commitment to the Dhamma, ordinarily wears white clothing during dedicated training, practices celibacy, participates regularly in meditation and Dhamma study, and progressively simplifies worldly life. Although the practitioner may retain certain household responsibilities, spiritual cultivation increasingly becomes the organizing principle of daily life.

- **70% --- Sāmaṇera / Sāmaṇerī:** A practitioner whose commitment to renunciation has become sufficiently strong and stable to undertake novice ordination. At this level, the practitioner is expected to relinquish the household way of life, wearing corresponding monastic robe, observe the Ten Training Precepts, accept the guidance of preceptor and teacher, and devote the principal part of daily life to monastic discipline, meditation, and the study of Dhamma.

- **80--90% --- Bhikkhu / Bhikkhunī:** A novice whose renunciation has matured sufficiently, and remained stable over time, to warrant consideration for full ordination. Full ordination requires the formal qualifications and procedures prescribed by the applicable Vinaya. Following ordination, the monastic is expected to maintain a high and sustained level of renunciation while undertaking only those communal, educational, and administrative responsibilities compatible with the monastic vocation.

- **90--95% --- Deeply Established Renunciation:** A mature monastic whose life is organized almost entirely around the cultivation of *Sīla*, *Samādhi*, *Paññā*, and liberation (*vimutti*). Worldly involvement is kept to a minimum, and solitude, simplicity, and meditation receive priority. Limited engagement with the Sangha and lay community remains appropriate when required to sustain the Dhamma, guide practitioners, and fulfill legitimate monastic responsibilities.

- **100% --- Complete Withdrawal from Worldly Engagement (Not Recommended):** Complete withdrawal from all external engagement is not the recommended model of monastic life in the Arahanist Order. Under the Theravāda Vinaya, Bhikkhus and Bhikkhunīs depend on the material support of the lay community, while the Sangha sustains the spiritual welfare of that community through teaching the Dhamma, offering guidance, and providing appropriate blessings and instruction. This reciprocal relationship is an integral part of monastic life. Renunciation should therefore eliminate attachment to worldly affairs without requiring complete isolation from the people who support and benefit from the Dhamma. The ideal monastic maintains minimal but regular engagement with the Sangha and lay community while preserving the solitude, simplicity, and freedom necessary for meditation and progress toward Arahantship.

These percentages provide a common language for discussing the progression of renunciation within the Order. They are indicative rather than mechanically measurable quantities. Teachers should assess a practitioner\'s actual way of life, motivations, conduct, and sustained commitment rather than assign a percentage solely on the basis of outward appearances.

**5.2. Readiness for Ordination**

The Order ordinarily considers a renunciation level exceeding 50% a significant indication that a practitioner may be ready to consider formal renunciation of household life. At this stage, the teacher should discuss the practical implications of ordination, including celibacy, dependence on communal support, relinquishment of ordinary economic activity, and the responsibilities of monastic discipline.

A practitioner who has attained approximately 70% renunciation and demonstrated stability over time may be considered for Sāmaṇera or Sāmaṇerī ordination, provided that the other requirements for novice training have been satisfied.

Similarly, a Sāmaṇera or Sāmaṇerī whose renunciation has developed to approximately 80% and remained stable may be considered for Bhikkhu or Bhikkhunī ordination. The assessment must also establish sufficient maturity, familiarity with the relevant discipline, readiness for full monastic responsibilities, and fulfillment of the applicable Vinaya requirements.

These thresholds are intended to prevent premature ordination. The aspiration to become a monastic must be supported by an actual willingness to relinquish the household way of life. Ordination should follow a demonstrated capacity for renunciation rather than precede it in the expectation that the title or status of a monastic will automatically produce the necessary transformation.

**5.3. Worldly Engagement and the Decline of Renunciation**

After ordination, renunciation must be maintained through the organization of daily life. A monastic may gradually become involved in activities that draw attention away from the central purpose of monastic training. These may include excessive institutional administration, fundraising, management of charitable organizations or property, public promotion of the Dhamma, family obligations, social relationships, and other activities that reproduce the responsibilities and preoccupations of worldly life.

Some such activities may be necessary or beneficial. The teaching of Dhamma, the guidance of disciples, the maintenance of communal facilities, and the responsible administration of an institution can support the practice of the Sangha. Nevertheless, their value must be assessed in relation to the purpose of renunciation. An activity that serves the Dhamma institutionally may still become an obstacle to an individual monastic\'s spiritual cultivation when it occupies too much of their time and attention.

The Order therefore adopts the following internal guidelines for monitoring the maintenance of renunciation.

**For Sāmaṇeras and Sāmaṇerīs:**

- **80% or above:** The novice should ordinarily maintain a stable commitment to renunciation, with monastic training, meditation, Dhamma study, and discipline taking priority over external activities.

- **Approximately 60%:** If the novice\'s renunciation declines to this level through increasing worldly involvement, the teacher should intervene, discuss the causes, and issue an appropriate warning. The novice should be guided to reduce unnecessary responsibilities and restore the conditions necessary for monastic training.

- **Below 50%:** If the decline is sustained and the novice is no longer willing or able to maintain the renunciant way of life, the teacher should formally recommend consideration of disrobing. The purpose is to prevent the continuation of a monastic identity that no longer corresponds to the practitioner\'s actual commitments.

**For Bhikkhus and Bhikkhunīs:**

- **80--95%:** A fully ordained monastic should ordinarily maintain renunciation within this range, with the precise balance between solitude, meditation, communal participation, and Dhamma service depending on individual circumstances and responsibilities.

- **Approximately 60%:** If renunciation declines to this level because worldly activities have become excessive, the teacher or supervising senior monastic should intervene, issue an appropriate warning, and help the monastic reorganize their life around the purpose of ordination.

- **Below 50%:** If the decline is sustained and the monastic is no longer willing or able to maintain the renunciant life, the teacher should recommend serious consideration of disrobing rather than allowing the monastic to continue indefinitely in a condition fundamentally inconsistent with their chosen vocation.

These thresholds are internal standards for guidance and review, not automatic judgments of spiritual worth or substitutes for formal disciplinary procedures. Before reaching a conclusion, the teacher should consider the nature of the responsibilities involved, the reasons for undertaking them, their actual effect on spiritual practice, and whether the monastic is willing to make the necessary changes.

A temporary increase in responsibilities does not necessarily indicate a loss of renunciation. A monastic may undertake exceptional duties for the welfare of the Sangha or the preservation of the Dhamma. The decisive question is whether these responsibilities remain subordinate to the purpose of renunciation or have gradually displaced it.

**5.4. The Proper Relationship Between Renunciation and Dhamma Service**

The Arahanist Order does not require every monastic to live as a complete hermit. The Sangha must maintain sufficient relationships to support communal discipline, preserve the teaching, guide sincere practitioners, and provide appropriate instruction in the Dhamma. Meditation teaching, the transmission of canonical knowledge, and necessary participation in Sangha affairs can form legitimate parts of monastic life.

However, service must remain subordinate to spiritual cultivation. A monastic who spends most of their time managing a charity, acquiring property, maintaining social relationships, organizing public events, or promoting an institution, risks exchanging the responsibilities of worldly life for institutional responsibilities without achieving the corresponding depth of renunciation.

The Order therefore encourages monastics to maintain only the degree of external engagement necessary for their vocation. As a practical guideline for *bhikkhu* or *bhikkhunī*, approximately 5--20% of one\'s time and energy may be devoted to essential engagement with the Sangha and lay community, while the remaining 80--95% is directed toward personal cultivation, meditation, study, and the maintenance of monastic discipline. This allocation is an aspirational guideline for planning one\'s life, not a canonical rule or a universal requirement. Actual responsibilities will vary according to the monastic\'s circumstances and duties.

The purpose is not to prohibit all social contact, teaching, or institutional responsibility. It is to prevent these activities from becoming the dominant organizing principle of monastic life. A monastic should remain connected to the community where appropriate while preserving sufficient solitude and freedom from worldly obligations to pursue the path toward liberation from worldly bondage.

**5.5. The Teacher\'s Responsibility**

The preceptor and teacher have a responsibility to protect the purpose of renunciation throughout the course of formation. Their role is not limited to granting ordination or instructing the student in formal discipline. They must also help practitioners recognize when their actual way of life has begun to diverge from the commitments they have undertaken.

The teacher should therefore review the practitioner\'s progress periodically, paying attention to the balance between meditation and external activity, the growth or decline of worldly attachments, the management of personal and institutional responsibilities, and the practitioner\'s willingness to simplify life when necessary.

When a decline becomes evident, the teacher should first seek to understand its causes and offer practical guidance. If the practitioner continues to accumulate worldly responsibilities despite repeated instruction, firmer intervention may be necessary. Where the renunciant vocation is no longer sustainable, the possibility of disrobing should be discussed openly and responsibly.

Such intervention is intended to preserve the integrity of the path, not to humiliate or punish the practitioner. Disrobing, when appropriate, should be understood as an honest recognition that the person\'s present circumstances or aspirations no longer accord with monastic life. Remaining in robes while abandoning the substance of renunciation does not serve the practitioner, the Sangha, or the Dhamma.

**5.6. Renunciation as the Governing Principle of Formation**

The Renunciation Level Framework expresses a central principle of Arahanist formation: advancement in religious status must be accompanied by a corresponding deepening of renunciation. A higher ordination does not excuse greater involvement in worldly affairs. On the contrary, it entails a more sustained commitment to the discipline and purpose of the renunciant life.

The ultimate objective is not to attain a particular percentage, preserve a title, or satisfy an institutional standard. It is to cultivate the conditions for liberation from worldly bondage through ethical discipline, samatha, vipassanā, wisdom, and the progressive relinquishment of worldly attachment.

The Order therefore uses the framework as an instrument of reflection, guidance, and accountability. Its percentages help make expectations explicit, but the essential question remains whether the practitioner is actually simplifying life, strengthening the mind, and advancing toward liberation from worldly bondage.

**Ordination Guidelines & Protocol**

## Upāsaka • Upāsikā Ordination

**Arahanist Order of Canada**

**Introduction: The Meaning of Upāsaka/Upāsikā Ordination**

Upāsaka/Upāsikā Ordination is a formal ceremony through which a committed lay practitioner enters a long-term course of spiritual training under the guidance of the Arahanist Order of Canada. It is intended for those who aspire to devote themselves to the systematic cultivation of Sīla, Samādhi, and Paññā while living at or maintaining a close, continuing relationship with the monastery.

This ordination is distinct from a temporary observance of the Eight Precepts. It marks a sustained commitment to the renunciant path, with an emphasis on ethical discipline, meditation, Dhamma study, sense restraint, and the development of inner happiness independent of worldly pleasures.

The candidate undertakes the Eight Precepts and agrees to follow the guidance of the appointed teacher and the resident monastic community. The ceremony is conducted formally by the monastery\'s resident monastics, who receive the candidate\'s commitment and establish the basis for continuing spiritual instruction.

An ordained Upāsaka or Upāsikā remains a lay practitioner and does not receive monastic status. Instead of monastic robes, the candidate wears white yogi clothing, signifying a life increasingly directed toward purity, simplicity, discipline, meditation, and renunciation.

The purpose of this ordination is not merely to adopt a religious identity. It is to establish a stable foundation for sustained spiritual practice and to prepare the practitioner for deeper renunciation according to their capacity and circumstances.

### Part I. Preparation for Ordination

#### **1. Understanding the Commitment**

Before ordination, the candidate should understand the meaning and responsibilities of becoming an Upāsaka or Upāsikā within the Arahanist Order.

This commitment includes:

- Undertaking the Eight Precepts as the foundation of daily conduct.

- Maintaining white yogi clothing as the customary dress of the ordained lay practitioner.

- Participating regularly in meditation, Dhamma instruction, and other assigned training.

- Practising restraint in speech, conduct, sensory enjoyment, and personal habits.

- Cultivating Samatha and Vipassanā under appropriate guidance.

- Accepting instruction and correction from the appointed teacher and cooperating respectfully with the resident monastic community.

- Maintaining a long-term commitment to the spiritual life rather than treating ordination as a temporary retreat experience.

The candidate should understand that this ordination is a serious spiritual undertaking. It calls for consistency, patience, humility, and a willingness to reorganize daily life around the cultivation of liberation.

#### **2. Confirmation of Readiness**

Before the ceremony, the teacher should meet with the candidate to discuss their motivation, understanding of the Eight Precepts, practical circumstances, and readiness for sustained training.

The teacher should explain the expectations of residence, daily practice, participation in monastery life, and continuing instruction. Where the candidate will reside at the monastery, practical arrangements should be established in advance.

The purpose of this preparation is to ensure that the candidate enters ordination with a clear understanding of the commitment being undertaken.

#### **3. Clothing and Personal Requisites**

The candidate should prepare a clean set of white yogi clothing suitable for meditation and monastery life.

White yogi clothing is not monastic robe attire. It is the customary clothing of the ordained lay practitioner and expresses simplicity, restraint, and commitment to spiritual training.

The candidate should also prepare any personal requisites requested by the monastery. Clothing and personal possessions should be simple and appropriate to a disciplined environment.

#### **4. Preparation of the Ceremony**

The ceremony should take place in the monastery\'s Dhamma hall or another suitable place of practice. The resident monastics should be present to witness and conduct the ordination according to the monastery\'s established procedure.

The candidate should arrive clean and prepared, wearing or carrying the white yogi clothing designated for the ceremony. The altar and ceremonial space should be arranged beforehand.

### Part II. The Upāsaka/Upāsikā Ordination Ceremony

#### **1. Entrance and Homage**

The candidate enters the Dhamma hall and pays respect to the resident monastics. The ceremony begins with an atmosphere of composure, reverence, and recollection of the purpose of renunciation.

The presiding monastic welcomes the candidate and briefly explains that the ceremony marks the formal beginning of a sustained course of lay spiritual training within the Arahanist Order.

#### **2. Formal Request for Ordination**

The candidate requests ordination three times.

Candidate: **Okāsa, ahaṃ, bhante, Upāsaka/Upāsikā yācāmi.**

*Permit me, Bhante. I request Upāsaka/Upāsikā ordination.*

**Dutiyam\'pi, ahaṃ, bhante, Upāsaka/Upāsikā yācāmi.**

*For the second time, Bhante, I request Upāsaka/Upāsikā ordination.*

**Tatiyam\'pi, ahaṃ, bhante, Upāsaka/Upāsikā yācāmi.**

*For the third time, Bhante, I request Upāsaka/Upāsikā ordination.*

The candidate then makes the request for Upāsaka/Upāsikā, repeated three times.

Candidate: **Sabba-dukkha-nissaraṇa-nibbāna-sacchi-karaṇatthāya**, **Upāsaka/Upāsikā maṃ, bhante, anukampaṃ upādāya.**

*For the release from all dukkha and for the purpose of directly realizing Nibbāna, please ordain me Upāsaka/Upāsikā, Bhante, out of compassion.*

**Second request: Dutiyam\'pi, sabba-dukkha-nissaraṇa-nibbāna-sacchi-karaṇatthāya, Upāsaka/Upāsikā maṃ, bhante, anukampaṃ upādāya.**

*For the second time, for the release from all dukkha and for the purpose of directly realizing Nibbāna, please ordain me Upāsaka/Upāsikā, Bhante, out of compassion.*

**Third request: Tatiyam\'pi, sabba-dukkha-nissaraṇa-nibbāna-sacchi-karaṇatthāya, Upāsaka/Upāsikā maṃ, bhante, anukampaṃ upādāya.**

*For the third time, for the release from all dukkha and for the purpose of directly realizing Nibbāna, please ordain me Upāsaka/Upāsikā, Bhante, out of compassion.*

After the requests, the candidate places the robes before the preceptor as directed.

#### **3. Acceptance by the Resident Monastics**

The presiding monastic confirms that the candidate has received the necessary instruction and understands the commitment.

The resident monastics formally accept the candidate\'s undertaking into the community\'s programme of Upāsaka/Upāsikā training. The presiding monastic may then offer a brief admonition on the purpose of renunciation, the importance of keeping the precepts, and the necessity of diligent practice.

This acceptance establishes the candidate\'s formal relationship with the monastery and its training community.

#### **4. Taking Three Refuges • Tisarana**

The candidate takes refuge in the Buddha, Dhamma, and Saṅgha, reciting the traditional formula after the presiding monastic.

**Namo Tassa Bhagavato Arahato Sammāsambuddhasa (X3)**

**Buddhaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Dhammaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Saṁghaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Dutiyampi Buddhaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Dutiyampi Dhammaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Dutiyampi Saṁghaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Tatiyampi Buddhaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Tatiyampi Dhammaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Tatiyampi Saṁghaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

\[**Bhante**\] **Tisaraṇa Gamanaṁ Sam-Puṇṇaṁ.** *Taking refuge is complete.*

**\[Student\] Āma Bhante.** *Yes, Bhante.*

The Three Refuges establish the spiritual orientation of the candidate. They express confidence in the path of liberation, the teaching that leads toward liberation, and the community that preserves and practises that teaching.

#### **5. Undertaking the Eight Precepts • Aṭṭhaṅga Sīla** 

The presiding monastic administers the Eight Precepts, and the candidate repeats each training rule.

**1. Pāṇātipātā veramaṇī, sikkhāpadaṃ samādiyāmi**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from destroying the life force.*

**2. Adinnā-dānā veramanī, sikkhāpadaṃ samādiyāmi**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from taking what is not given.*

**3. Abrahma-cariyā veramanī, sikkhāpadaṃ samādiyāmi**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from Abrahmacariya conduct.*

**4. Musā-vādā veramanī, sikkhāpadaṃ samādiyāmi**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from false speech.*

**5. Surā-meraya-majja-pamā-daṭṭhānā veramanī, sikkhāpadaṃ samādiyāmi**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from fermented drink, distilled liquor, and intoxicating substances---the ground of spiritual laxity.*

**6. Vikāla-bhojanā veramaṇī, sikkhāpadaṃ samādiyāmi**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from taking food at improper times.*

**7. Nacca-gīta-vādita-visūka-dassanā mālā-gandha-vilepana-dhāraṇa-maṇḍana-vibhūsanaṭṭhānā veramaṇī, sikkhāpadaṃ samādiyāmi**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from dancing, singing, playing music, and distractive shows and music; and wearing garlands, perfumes, cosmetics, for the purpose of beautification and ornamentation.*

**8. Uccā-sayana mahā-sayanā veramaṇī, sikkhāpadaṃ samādiyāmi.**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from high beds and luxurious beds.*

**\[Bhante\]** **Tisaranena saddhim Aṭṭhaṅga Samannāgatam sīlaṁ dhammam sādhukam surakkhitam katvā Appamādena sampādethabbam.**

*Maintaining well the Eight Precepts that lead to Samannā together with the Three Refuges, strive on with diligence.*

**\[Student\] Āma Bhante.** *Yes, bhante.*

The Eight Precepts are not merely ceremonial declarations. They establish the discipline through which the practitioner reduces dependence on sensory pleasure, simplifies daily life, and creates favourable conditions for meditation and insight.

#### **6. Formal Declaration of Commitment**

After undertaking the Eight Precepts, the candidate makes a declaration of long-term commitment.

The following English declaration may be used:

**I undertake to live as an Upāsaka/Upāsikā under the Arahanist Order of Canada, maintaining the Eight Precepts and wearing white yogi clothing as the customary expression of this commitment. I will cultivate Sīla, Samādhi, and Paññā; train diligently in meditation; study and reflect upon the Dhamma; and follow the guidance of my appointed teacher and the resident monastic community. I understand that this ordination establishes a continuing commitment to spiritual training and the cultivation of the path leading toward liberation from worldly bondage.**

The candidate then pays respect to the presiding monastic and the resident monastics.

#### **7. Receiving a Pāli Name**

The preceptor gives the Upāsaka/Upāsikā a Pāli monastic name, according to the naming customs of the Arahanist Order of Canada.

The name may serve as a reminder of the qualities the Upāsaka/Upāsikā is encouraged to cultivate or of the spiritual direction of the renunciant life.

The preceptor announces the name clearly and explains its meaning where appropriate. The Upāsaka/Upāsikā should be instructed in the correct pronunciation and use of the name within the renunciant community.

#### **8. Final Admonition and Blessing**

The presiding monastic offers a final instruction, emphasizing the following principles:

- Keep the Eight Precepts sincerely and consistently.

- Practise meditation regularly rather than relying on occasional periods of enthusiasm.

- Cultivate Samatha to develop stability, collectedness, and concentration of mind.

- Cultivate Vipassanā to investigate experience with wisdom and develop disenchantment with attachment.

- Simplify personal habits and reduce unnecessary dependence on worldly pleasures.

- Maintain respect for the teacher, resident monastics, and fellow practitioners.

- Regard inner happiness, concentration, and the development of wisdom as essential foundations of renunciation.

- Treat ordination as the beginning of sustained training, not as the completion of spiritual development.

The ceremony concludes with homage and a blessing for the candidate\'s diligent progress on the path.

### Part III. The Life and Training of an Ordained Upāsaka/Upāsikā

#### **1. Relationship with the Teacher and Monastery**

An ordained Upāsaka/Upāsikā trains under an appointed teacher and maintains a continuing relationship with the resident monastic community. The teacher provides instruction, reviews the practitioner\'s progress, and guides the development of meditation and understanding.

The teacher is responsible for spiritual guidance within the scope of the role established by the monastery. The Upāsaka/Upāsikā remains a lay practitioner and is not appointed as a Bhikkhu\'s or Bhikkhunī\'s monastic disciple through formal monastic ordination.

#### **2. Daily Discipline**

The practitioner should follow an established daily routine suited to the monastery\'s training programme. This may include:

- Morning and evening meditation.

- Formal Samatha and Vipassanā practice.

- Dhamma study and reflection.

- Participation in appropriate communal activities.

- Observance of the Eight Precepts.

- Periods of silence and restraint where prescribed.

- Regular meetings with the teacher to review practice.

Where the practitioner resides at the monastery, the daily schedule should support a stable environment for spiritual development. Personal conduct should be guided by simplicity, consideration for others, and respect for the monastery\'s discipline.

#### **3. Sīla: The Foundation of Training**

Sīla establishes the ethical foundation for Samādhi and Paññā. The Eight Precepts help the practitioner restrain bodily and verbal conduct, simplify daily life, and reduce activities that agitate the mind.

The practitioner should learn to observe not only outward conduct but also the intentions and habits that give rise to unwholesome actions. Ethical discipline becomes increasingly meaningful when it supports inner composure and the gradual abandonment of craving, aversion, and delusion.

#### **4. Samatha: The Development of Collectedness**

Samatha training develops concentration, stability, and concentration. Through systematic practice under the teacher\'s guidance, the practitioner learns to gather the mind, sustain attention, and reduce agitation and distraction.

The purpose is not merely temporary relaxation. A collected mind provides a stronger basis for investigating experience and understanding the conditions that sustain attachment.

#### **5. Vipassanā: The Development of Insight**

Vipassanā training cultivates direct investigation of experience. The practitioner learns to observe bodily and mental phenomena, recognize their changing and conditioned character, and understand how attachment and identification contribute to suffering.

Samatha and Vipassanā support one another. Concentration steadies the mind, while insight clarifies the nature of experience. Both belong within a systematic course of training directed toward disenchantment, dispassion, and liberation.

#### **6. Inner Happiness and Renunciation**

The practitioner should gradually learn to derive happiness from ethical conduct, meditation, concentration, and the abandonment of unnecessary desires.

Renunciation does not mean cultivating a hostile attitude toward the world. It means learning to live without making worldly pleasures the foundation of one\'s happiness. As the mind becomes more composed and independent of external stimulation, the practitioner can devote greater energy to meditation, reflection, and the cultivation of wisdom.

#### **7. Continuing Development**

Upāsaka/Upāsikā Ordination establishes a basis for long-term training. Progress depends on sustained practice, honest self-examination, and appropriate guidance.

The practitioner should not regard the ceremony itself as evidence of spiritual attainment. The significance of the commitment is expressed through the gradual development of conduct, collectedness, wisdom, and freedom from attachment.

### Part IV. Continuing Commitment and Review

#### **1. Maintaining the Ordained Commitment**

The Upāsaka/Upāsikā should maintain the Eight Precepts in accordance with the commitment undertaken and the monastery\'s established training rules.

Because this is a long-term commitment, the monastery should explain how precept observance, periods of illness or difficulty, travel, and other practical circumstances are to be handled. Any changes to the commitment should be discussed with the appointed teacher rather than left ambiguous.

#### **2. Regular Guidance**

The practitioner should meet with the teacher at agreed intervals to review ethical conduct, meditation practice, Dhamma study, and the practical challenges of living a disciplined spiritual life. These meetings help ensure that the ordination remains connected to actual training rather than becoming only a title or outward identity.

#### **3. Distinction from Temporary Uposatha Observance**

A temporary Uposatha observance of the Eight Precepts may last for a day, several days, or another specified period. Upāsaka/Upāsikā Ordination, as established by the Arahanist Order, is intended to signify a continuing commitment to spiritual training. The monastery should clearly communicate this distinction to every candidate before the ceremony.

#### **4. Distinction from Monastic Ordination**

Upāsaka/Upāsikā Ordination is a formal lay ordination within the Arahanist Order\'s training framework. It does not confer the status of Sāmaṇera, Sāmaṇerī, Bhikkhu, or Bhikkhunī.

The ordained lay practitioner wears white yogi clothing rather than monastic robes and undertakes the Eight Precepts rather than the full monastic discipline. The purpose is to establish a disciplined form of lay renunciation while preserving the distinction between lay and monastic life.

**Concluding Reflection**

Upāsaka/Upāsikā Ordination is a formal commitment to a life increasingly directed toward spiritual cultivation. Through the Eight Precepts, white yogi clothing, systematic meditation, Dhamma study, and continuing guidance from the teacher and resident monastics, the practitioner establishes a foundation for sustained progress on the path of liberation.

The ceremony marks the beginning of this commitment. Its deeper meaning unfolds through daily conduct, the development of Samatha, the investigation of experience through Vipassanā, and the gradual weakening of attachment to worldly pleasures.

The ordained Upāsaka/Upāsikā remains a lay practitioner, yet undertakes a disciplined course of training that goes beyond occasional religious observance. By maintaining ethical restraint, cultivating inner concentration, and developing wisdom, the practitioner prepares the conditions for deeper renunciation and further progress toward liberation.

**Ordination Guidelines & Protocol**

## PABBAJJĀ: Sāmaṇera • Sāmaṇerī Novice Ordination

**Arahanist Order of Canada**

**Introduction**

Pabbajjā marks the beginning of formal monastic life. Through this ordination, a candidate leaves the ordinary household way of life and undertakes the discipline of a novice renunciant.

The purpose of Pabbajjā extends beyond wearing the robes or adopting a new religious identity. It is a commitment to a life directed toward liberation from worldly bondage: abandoning unnecessary worldly concerns, cultivating virtue, developing concentration, investigating the nature of worldly existence, and following the path leading toward Arahantship.

The Sāmaṇera or Sāmaṇerī undertakes the Three Refuges and the Ten Training Precepts, places themselves under the guidance of a preceptor and teacher, and begins systematic training in monastic conduct, Dhamma, and meditation.

The ordination ceremony should therefore be conducted with dignity, composure, and a clear understanding of its spiritual significance. Every stage---from the candidate\'s initial preparation to the final instructions---should help establish the foundation for a life of renunciation.

These guidelines establish a consistent ceremonial sequence for novice ordination within the Arahanist Order of Canada. They are intended to guide candidates, preceptors, assisting monastics, and those responsible for preparing the ordination gathering.

### Restoring the True Meaning of Pabbajjā: A Formal Arahanist Declaration of Renunciation

#### 1. The Problem of Translating Pabbajjā as \"Going Forth\"

In conventional Buddhist literature and modern academic scholarship, the Pāli term *Pabbajjā*, corresponding to Sanskrit *Pravrajyā*, is commonly translated into English as \"going forth.\" This rendering follows the word\'s assumed and supposed etymological construction, conveying the sense of departing or setting out.

Although this translation may reflect the assumed "literal linguistic expression", it does not adequately communicate the religious significance of the act within Gautama\'s liberation teaching.

What does a person actually undertake when entering *Pabbajjā*? Does one merely go forth from somewhere, or does one make a decisive renunciation of household existence and undertake an entirely different way of life dedicated to liberation from worldly bondage?

The answer is fundamental.

*Pabbajjā* marks a decisive turning point in an individual\'s life. It is the abandonment of household existence and the undertaking of the renunciant path. A person leaves behind domestic life, family relationships, ordinary social engagements, worldly responsibilities, and the familiar conditions of household existence to pursue the ultimate spiritual objective: liberation from the worldly control.

The expression \"going forth\" may describe the movement. It does not adequately express the renunciation that gives this movement its religious significance.

A translation that may preserve the surface expression while obscuring the essential purpose of the act is insufficient for communicating the true Dhamma of Gautama.

#### 2. The Primacy of Renunciation in Gautama\'s Liberation Teaching

Renunciation occupies a central position in the life and teaching of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.

His own departure from household life was not an ordinary journey or a change of residence. It was a radical abandonment of worldly existence in pursuit of the Deathless. The moment he relinquished the household life to undertake the spiritual quest, he is already a Buddha, an Awakened human being.

This renunciant commitment is reflected in the recurring Pāli expression: *Agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajati.*

"One renounces household life and enters the homeless life."

The contrast between *agāra* (household) and *anagāriya* (non-householder existence) is central. The expression identifies a fundamental transformation in the individual\'s way of life.

The person undertaking *Pabbajjā* leaves the household condition and enters the life of a renunciant. This is a deliberate commitment to spiritual discipline, sense restraint, meditation, liberating wisdom, and the eradication of the defilements.

Renunciation is therefore not an incidental feature of Buddhist practice. It is the foundation of the full renunciant quest for liberation from worldly bondage and control.

To understand *Pabbajjā* merely as \"going forth\" has already reduced this decisive spiritual commitment to a description of departure. The essential meaning must remain visible: the individual renounces household existence to pursue liberation from worldly bondage and control.

#### 3. The Limitations of Mainstream Academic Translation

The conventional translation of *Pabbajjā* as \"going forth\" illustrates a broader problem in the translation and interpretation of early "Buddhist" terminology.

Modern academic scholarship frequently gives priority to etymological reconstruction, lexical classification, and historical linguistic analysis. These methods are valuable, but they cannot independently determine the full doctrinal significance of a religious expression.

A word\'s etymological construction and its established religious function are related, but they are not necessarily identical.

In the case of *Pabbajjā*, the literal idea of going forth must be understood within the religious context of leaving household life and undertaking renunciation. When the assumed "literal expression" becomes the principal English translation without adequately conveying this defining purpose, the reader receives an incomplete understanding of the practice.

The problem becomes more consequential when such translations are repeatedly reproduced in dictionaries, academic publications, educational materials, and Buddhist institutions. Terminological choices influence how subsequent generations understand Gautama Buddha\'s path.

If renunciation is linguistically and conceptually obscured, the significance of leaving household existence can gradually recede from the reader\'s understanding. The renunciant life may then appear as one optional religious lifestyle among many, rather than a decisive commitment to the full liberation path.

Arahanism challenges this interpretive reductionism.

The Dhamma must be understood according to its liberative purpose. Linguistic analysis must serve that understanding, not displace it.

#### 4. The Secularization and Reduction of Liberation

The problem of terminology cannot be separated from the broader development of modern interpretations of Buddhism.

Across contemporary religious and academic discourse, Gautama Buddha\'s teaching is frequently presented through psychological, therapeutic, philosophical, and secular frameworks. Suffering is often discussed primarily in terms of psychological distress, emotional disturbance, dissatisfaction, or negative mental states. Liberation is consequently presented as a form of psychological well-being, emotional balance, or freedom from personal negativity.

These dimensions may be relevant to ordinary human experience, but they do not reflect Gautama\'s true liberation teaching.

The ultimate objective of the renunciant path is not simply to become psychologically healthier, manage difficult emotions, or develop a more satisfactory relationship with worldly existence.

The objective is liberation from worldly bondage through the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, and the cessation of the conditions sustaining renewed worldly existence or manifestation. This is the distinction between psychological improvement and liberation from worldly bondage.

When the renunciant dimension of the teaching is weakened, the meaning of liberation itself is also diminished. A teaching directed towards the complete transcendence of worldly bondage can be transformed into a system for improving one\'s experience within the world.

Arahanism rejects this reduction.

The restoration of the meaning of *Pabbajjā* is therefore part of a larger effort to restore the centrality of renunciation, the full training of the individual, and the attainment of liberation as understood in Gautama\'s original teaching.

#### 5. Pabbajjā as the Beginning of the Renunciant Training

Within Arahanist Training, *Pabbajjā* represents a decisive stage in the individual\'s spiritual development.

It marks the transition from household existence into the renunciant life. The individual undertakes a disciplined commitment to abandon worldly attachments, observe the monastic training, cultivate *samādhi*, develop *paññā*, and progressively eradicate the defilements.

This commitment must be understood in its full significance. Renunciation involves a transformation of one\'s entire orientation towards existence. The individual no longer organises life around household responsibilities, material acquisition, family expectations, social recognition, or worldly advancement. The renunciant life is directed towards the purification of *citta* and the attainment of liberation from worldly bondage.

Within the Arahanist training structure, the stages of commitment must remain clearly distinguished:

- **Pabbajjā --- Renunciation:** Leaving household existence and undertaking the renunciant training.

- **Upasampadā --- Higher Ordination:** Formal admission into Gautama's lineage: the ***Saṅgha***, the full status and responsibilities of Bhikkhu or Bhikkhunī.

Pabbajjā is therefore not merely an administrative preliminary to higher ordination. It is itself a profound spiritual commitment and a defining threshold in the liberation journey.

#### 6. The Official Arahanist Translation: Pabbajjā --- Renunciation

Arahanism hereby establishes **Renunciation** as the official English translation of *Pabbajjā*.

The conventional rendering \"going forth\" will not be adopted as the principal English translation in Arahanist publications, educational materials, institutional documents, or descriptions of Arahanist Training.

The Pāli term *Pabbajjā* will be retained, accompanied by its official English rendering:

**Pabbajjā --- Renunciation**

This terminology communicates the defining religious significance of the practice: the abandonment of household existence and the undertaking of a life devoted to spiritual purification and liberation from worldly bondage.

This declaration does not deny the historical linguistic relationship between *Pabbajjā* and the expression \"going forth.\" It establishes that Arahanism will not allow the etymological expression to displace the essential meaning of the renunciant commitment.

The purpose of translation is to communicate the teaching. Where a conventional rendering fails to convey the central significance of a practice, its adequacy must be challenged.

#### 7. A New Era of Renunciant Restoration

The restoration of *Pabbajjā* as Renunciation is part of the wider Arahanist movement to restore the original liberation teaching of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.

For too long, the radical significance of renunciation has been obscured by interpretations that accommodate Gautama\'s true teaching to worldly expectations, secular interests, and psychological reductionism.

Arahanism calls for a restoration of the original spiritual priority: the individual must undertake the path of purification, abandon worldly bondage, and pursue the complete eradication of the defilements.

The renunciant life is not an accessory to this objective. It is the dedicated way of life through which the full training towards Arahantship is undertaken.

The meaning of *Pabbajjā* must therefore be expressed with clarity. It is Renunciation.

A person undertaking *Pabbajjā* does not merely go forth. That person renounces household existence, leaves behind the familiar foundations of worldly life, and commits to the spiritual quest for liberation.

This is the significance that Arahanism restores.

**Pabbajjā is Renunciation. Renunciation is the threshold of the full renunciant path. The objective of that path is liberation from worldly bondage and the attainment of the Deathless.**

This is the Arahanist declaration.

### Part I. Preparation for Ordination

#### **1. Understanding the Commitment**

Before requesting Pabbajjā, a candidate should receive instruction concerning the purpose and responsibilities of novice life.

The candidate should understand that renunciation involves more than changing clothing or residing in a monastery. It requires a sincere willingness to simplify one\'s life, observe the Ten Training Precepts, accept guidance, and devote oneself to spiritual development.

The candidate should be prepared to undertake:

- The Three Refuges as the foundation of spiritual commitment.

- The Ten Training Precepts governing novice conduct.

- Training under the guidance of a preceptor and teacher.

- The study of Dhamma and the practice of meditation.

- A life of simplicity, sense restraint, contentment, and disciplined conduct.

- The gradual development of the qualities necessary for progress toward Arahantship.

The preceptor should ensure that the candidate has a reasonable understanding of these commitments before the ordination date is confirmed.

#### **2. Personal Responsibilities and Readiness**

Before ordination, the candidate should review and settle responsibilities that may conflict with monastic life.

These may include:

- Family and dependent-care responsibilities.

- Employment, business, and financial commitments.

- Outstanding debts, legal proceedings, or other unresolved obligations.

- Ownership or management of property, investments, and possessions.

- Necessary consent or authorization, including parental or guardian consent where applicable.

- Health considerations that may affect the candidate\'s ability to participate in and sustain monastic training.

The candidate should discuss relevant circumstances honestly with the preceptor. The purpose of this preparation is to ensure that the decision to renounce is responsible, informed, and compatible with the candidate\'s circumstances and the applicable requirements.

Any eligibility restrictions or required permissions should be reviewed before the ceremony. Where the candidate\'s circumstances require additional consideration, the preceptor should resolve the matter before confirming the ordination.

#### **3. Confirmation of the Ordination**

Once the candidate has been accepted for training and the necessary preparations have been completed, the candidate and preceptor should agree upon the date, place, and arrangements for the ceremony.

The preceptor should confirm:

1.  The candidate\'s readiness for novice life.

2.  The availability of the preceptor and any assisting monastics.

3.  The preparation of the robes, alms bowl, and other necessary requisites.

4.  The arrangement of the ordination space.

5.  The availability of the Pāli recitations and ceremonial instructions.

6.  The arrangements for the candidate\'s immediate training following ordination.

The ceremony should take place in a clean, quiet, and suitably prepared setting. The atmosphere should support reverence, concentration, and reflection.

#### **4. Required Requisites**

The following items should be prepared before the ceremony:

- A complete set of novice robes.

- An alms bowl.

- A suitable tray or arrangement for offerings.

- White clothing for the candidate before ordination.

- A clean and appropriate space for the ceremony.

- A printed or otherwise accessible copy of the Pāli recitations.

- Any additional requisites considered necessary by the preceptor.

The assisting monastics should check that the robes and other requisites are ready before the candidate enters.

#### **5. Preparation of the Candidate**

On the day of ordination, the candidate should have the head and beard shaved, as appropriate to the tradition and circumstances, and change into clean white clothing.

White clothing represents the candidate\'s condition before taking on the robes of renunciation. The candidate should approach the ceremony with humility and a sincere intention to begin a disciplined spiritual life.

The candidate should carry the prepared set of robes on the forearms, with the hands joined in a gesture of respect when appropriate.

Before entering the gathering, the candidate should be reminded of the ceremonial sequence and the importance of following the preceptor\'s instructions attentively.

### Part II. The Pabbajjā Ceremony

#### **6. Entrance and Initial Homage**

The candidate enters the gathering carrying the robes and approaches the preceptor.

The candidate places the robes to the left and the offering tray to the right, or follows the arrangement directed by the preceptor. The candidate then presents the offerings to the preceptor and pays homage three times using the five-point prostration, with the forehead, two forearms, and two knees touching the floor.

After paying homage, the candidate remains kneeling, takes the robes onto the forearms again, and joins the hands respectfully in preparation for requesting Pabbajjā.

The preceptor should allow sufficient time for the candidate to settle physically and mentally before beginning the recitations.

#### **7. Request for Pabbajjā Ordination**

The candidate requests ordination three times.

**Candidate: Okāsa, ahaṃ, bhante, pabbajjaṃ yācāmi.**

*Permit me, Bhante. I request Pabbajjā ordination.*

**Dutiyam\'pi, ahaṃ, bhante, pabbajjaṃ yācāmi.**

*For the second time, Bhante, I request Pabbajjā ordination.*

**Tatiyam\'pi, ahaṃ, bhante, pabbajjaṃ yācāmi.**

*For the third time, Bhante, I request Pabbajjā ordination.*

The candidate then makes request for renunciation, repeated three times.

**Candidate: Sabba-dukkha-nissaraṇa-nibbāna-sacchi-karaṇatthāya, etaṃ kāsāvaṃ datvā pabbājetha maṃ, bhante, anukampaṃ upādāya.**

*For the release from all dukkha and for the purpose of directly realizing Nibbāna, please give me these saffron robes and ordain me, Bhante, out of compassion.*

**Second request: Dutiyam\'pi, sabba-dukkha-nissaraṇa-nibbāna-sacchi-karaṇatthāya, etaṃ kāsāvaṃ datvā pabbājetha maṃ, bhante, anukampaṃ upādāya.**

*For the second time, for the release from all dukkha and for the purpose of directly realizing Nibbāna, please give me these saffron robes and ordain me, Bhante, out of compassion.*

**Third request: Tatiyam\'pi, sabba-dukkha-nissaraṇa-nibbāna-sacchi-karaṇatthāya, etaṃ kāsāvaṃ datvā pabbājetha maṃ, bhante, anukampaṃ upādāya.**

*For the third time, for the release from all dukkha and for the purpose of directly realizing Nibbāna, please give me these saffron robes and ordain me, Bhante, out of compassion.*

After the requests, the candidate places the robes before the preceptor as directed.

#### **8. Instruction in the Three Refuges and the Purpose of Renunciation**

The preceptor explains the significance of the Three Refuges and the responsibilities of novice life. The candidate should be reminded that the robes symbolize a commitment to renunciation and spiritual training.

The preceptor should explain that Pabbajjā begins a course of disciplined practice. Its purpose is to establish the conditions for the cultivation of virtue, concentration, and wisdom, leading toward the eradication of defilements and the realization of liberation.

The preceptor then introduces the *Taca Pañcaka Kammaṭṭhāna*, the meditation on five bodily constituents traditionally taught at ordination.

**The Taca Pañcaka Kammaṭṭhāna**

The candidate repeats each word after the preceptor, first in the forward order and then in reverse order.

**Forward order: Kesā** - Head hair. **Lomā** - Body hair. **Nakhā** - Nails. **Dantā** - Teeth. **Taco** - Skin.

**Reverse order: Taco** - Skin. **Dantā** - Teeth. **Nakhā** - Nails. **Lomā** - Body hair. **Kesā** - Head hair.

The preceptor should explain the purpose of this contemplation in an accessible manner, introducing the examination of the body as a foundation for disenchantment with physical appearance and the development of insight.

The candidate should repeat the words attentively and learn to recollect them as part of subsequent training.

#### **9. Receiving the Robes**

After teaching the contemplation, the preceptor removes the appropriate shoulder cloth (*aṃsa*) from the robe set and places it over the candidate\'s head, covering the left shoulder according to the local ceremonial custom.

The preceptor returns the remaining robes and explains how they are to be worn.

The candidate receives the robes respectfully, keeps the hands joined where appropriate, and withdraws from the ceremonial area according to the preceptor\'s directions. If required by the established custom of the gathering, the candidate backs away on the knees before standing and proceeding to the place designated for changing. An assisting monastic accompanies the candidate and provides help with putting on the robes.

#### **10. Reflection on the Use of the Robes**

While putting on the robes, the candidate recites the reflection below.

**Paccavekkhaṇa: Reflection on the Robes**

**Paṭisaṅkhā yoniso cīvaraṃ paṭisevāmi, yāvadeva sītassa paṭighātāya, uṇhassa paṭighātāya, ḍaṃsa-makasa-vātātapa-siriṃsapa-samphassānaṃ paṭighātāya, yāvadeva hirikopīna-paṭicchādanatthaṃ.**

*Reflecting wisely, I use the robes only to protect myself from cold and heat, from gadflies, mosquitoes, wind, sun, and creeping creatures, and for the purpose of covering the parts of the body that should be concealed.*

This reflection establishes the proper attitude toward the robes. They are requisites for a life of renunciation, not ornaments for personal display. The novice should learn to use them with gratitude, restraint, and understanding. After dressing, the candidate returns to the gathering, pays homage to the Saṅgha as directed, and approaches the preceptor to request the Three Refuges and Ten Training Precepts.

### Part III. The Three Refuges and Ten Training Precepts

#### **11. Request for the Three Refuges and Ten Precepts**

The candidate kneels before the preceptor with hands joined respectfully and makes the request three times.

**Candidate: Okāsa! Ahaṃ, bhante, tisaraṇena saha dasa sāmaṇera/sāmaṇerī pabbajjā-sīlaṃ dhammaṃ yācāmi. Anuggahaṃ katvā sīlaṃ detha me, bhante.**

*Permit me, Bhante. I request the Dhamma and the Ten Training Precepts for Sāmaṇera/Sāmaṇerī renunciation, together with the Three Refuges. Out of compassion, please grant me these training precepts, Bhante.*

The candidate repeats the request: **Dutiyam\'pi, ahaṃ, bhante, tisaraṇena saha dasa sāmaṇera/sāmaṇerī pabbajjā-sīlaṃ dhammaṃ yācāmi. Anuggahaṃ katvā sīlaṃ detha me, bhante.**

*For the second time, Bhante, I request the Dhamma and the Ten Training Precepts for Sāmaṇera/Sāmaṇerī renunciation, together with the Three Refuges. Out of compassion, please grant me these training precepts, Bhante.*

The candidate repeats the request a third time: **Tatiyam\'pi, ahaṃ, bhante, tisaraṇena saha dasa sāmaṇera/sāmaṇerī pabbajjā-sīlaṃ dhammaṃ yācāmi. Anuggahaṃ katvā sīlaṃ detha me, bhante.**

*For the third time, Bhante, I request the Dhamma and the Ten Training Precepts for Sāmaṇera/Sāmaṇerī renunciation, together with the Three Refuges. Out of compassion, please grant me these training precepts, Bhante.*

The preceptor instructs the candidate to repeat the following recitations.

**Preceptor: Yaṃ ahaṃ vadāmi, taṃ vadehi.** (Repeat after me what I say.)

**Candidate: Āma, bhante.** (Yes, Bhante.)

#### **12. Taking the Three Refuges • Tisarana**

The candidate pays homage through the following formula, reciting it three times.

**Namo Tassa Bhagavato Arahato Sammāsambuddhasa (X3)**

**Buddhaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Dhammaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Saṁghaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Dutiyampi Buddhaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Dutiyampi Dhammaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Dutiyampi Saṁghaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Tatiyampi Buddhaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Tatiyampi Dhammaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

**Tatiyampi Saṁghaṁ Saranaṁ Gacchāmi.**

The preceptor announces the completion of the taking of refuge.

\[**Preceptor**\] **Tisaraṇa Gamanaṁ Sam-Puṇṇaṁ.** *Taking refuge is complete.*

**\[Candidate\] Āma Bhante.** *Yes, Bhante.*

#### **13. Taking the Ten Training Precepts**

The preceptor recites each training precept, and the candidate repeats it as instructed. The Ten Training Precepts establish the foundation of novice conduct. They guide the candidate away from harmful actions, sensory indulgence, and dependence on worldly possessions, preparing the mind for a life of simplicity, concentration, and insight.

**Precept 1: Refraining from Taking Life**

**Pāṇātipātā veramaṇī sikkhāpadaṃ.**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from destroying the life force.*

**Precept 2: Refraining from Taking What Is Not Given**

**Adinnādānā veramaṇī sikkhāpadaṃ.**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from taking what is not given.*

**Precept 3: Refraining from Sexual Activity**

**Abrahmacariyā veramaṇī sikkhāpadaṃ.**

*I undertake the training precept to abstain from sexual activity and to maintain celibacy.*

**Precept 4: Refraining from False Speech**

**Musāvādā veramaṇī sikkhāpadaṃ.**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from false speech.*

**Precept 5: Refraining from Intoxicants**

**Surā-meraya-majja-pamādaṭṭhānā veramaṇī sikkhāpadaṃ.**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from fermented drink, distilled liquor, and intoxicating substances---the ground of spiritual laxity.*

**Precept 6: Refraining from Eating at Improper Times**

**Vikālabhojanā veramaṇī sikkhāpadaṃ.**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from taking food at improper times.*

**Precept 7: Refraining from Entertainment and Performances**

**Nacca-gīta-vādita-visūka-dassanā veramaṇī sikkhāpadaṃ.**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from dancing, singing, playing music, and distractive shows and music.*

**Precept 8: Refraining from Adornment and Cosmetics**

**Mālā-gandha-vilepana-dhāraṇa-maṇḍana-vibhūsanaṭṭhānā veramaṇī sikkhāpadaṃ.**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from wearing garlands, perfumes, cosmetics, for the purpose of beautification and ornamentation.*

**Precept 9: Refraining from High and Luxurious Beds and Seats**

**Uccāsayana-mahāsayanā veramaṇī sikkhāpadaṃ.**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from high and luxurious beds and seats.*

**Precept 10: Refraining from Accepting Money**

**Jātarūpa-rajata-paṭiggahaṇā veramaṇī sikkhāpadaṃ.**

*I undertake the training step to abstain from accepting gold, silver, and money.*

**Formal Undertaking**

After receiving instruction in the ten precepts, the candidate undertakes them as a complete set, reciting the following formula three times.

**Candidate: Imāni pabbajjā-dasa-sikkhāpadāni samādiyāmi.**

*I undertake these Ten Training Precepts of renunciation.*

**Dutiyampi, imāni pabbajjā-dasa-sikkhāpadāni samādiyāmi.**

*For the second time, I undertake these Ten Training Precepts of renunciation.*

**Tatiyampi, imāni pabbajjā-dasa-sikkhāpadāni samādiyāmi.**

*For the third time, I undertake these Ten Training Precepts of renunciation.*

**The Preceptor\'s Admonition**

After the candidate has undertaken the precepts, the preceptor gives the following instruction.

**Preceptor: Tisaraṇena saddhiṃ pabbajjā-dasa-sīlaṃ dhammaṃ sādhukaṃ surakkhitaṃ katvā appamādena sampādehi.**

*Having undertaken the Ten Training Precepts of renunciation together with the Three Refuges, guard them carefully and fulfil your training with diligence.*

**Candidate: Āma, bhante.** (*Yes, Bhante.*)

The preceptor should briefly emphasize that the precepts are to be maintained through understanding and sustained practice. They are the foundation upon which the novice develops sense restraint, concentration, wisdom, and the capacity to progress toward liberation.

### Part IV. Establishing the Teacher--Student Relationship

#### **14. Requesting a Preceptor (Upajjhāya)**

A novice requires guidance in the proper conduct of monastic life. The candidate therefore formally requests a preceptor. The candidate kneels respectfully before the designated preceptor.

**Candidate: Okāsa! Upajjhāyo me, bhante, hohi.**

*Bhante, please be my preceptor.*

The request is repeated three times.

**Preceptor: Paṭirūpaṃ.** (*It is appropriate*.)

**Candidate: Sādhu, okāsa, sampaṭicchāmi***.* (*Very well, Bhante. I accept*.)

The preceptor\'s role is to guide the novice in discipline, conduct, study, meditation, and the responsibilities of renunciation.

#### **15. Requesting a Teacher (Ācariya)**

Where a separate teacher is designated, the candidate requests the guidance of an Ācariya.

**Candidate: Okāsa! Ācariyo me, bhante, hohi.**

*Bhante, please be my teacher.*

The request is repeated three times.

**Teacher: Paṭirūpaṃ***.* (*It is appropriate*.)

**Candidate: Sādhu, okāsa, sampaṭicchāmi.** (*Very well, Bhante. I accept*.)

The Ācariya assists in the novice\'s training and instruction according to the arrangements established by the Order.

Where the same monastic serves as both Upajjhāya and Ācariya, the preceptor should clarify the responsibilities being undertaken and follow the appropriate ceremonial form.

#### **16. Presentation of the Alms Bowl**

The novice is presented with the alms bowl.

The bowl is a practical requisite of monastic life and a reminder that the renunciant depends upon the generosity of others for material sustenance. This dependence calls for gratitude, restraint, and careful reflection on the proper use of requisites.

The preceptor should explain that the bowl is not merely a possession but an instrument of the renunciant life. The novice should learn to receive food without greed, use it appropriately, and cultivate contentment with what is offered.

The novice should also receive instruction in the proper handling, care, and use of the bowl according to the Order\'s customs.

#### **17. Receiving a Pāli Name**

The preceptor gives the novice a Pāli monastic name, if not already have or if want to change, according to the naming customs of the Arahanist Order of Canada.

The name may serve as a reminder of the qualities the novice is encouraged to cultivate or of the spiritual direction of the renunciant life.

The preceptor announces the name clearly and explains its meaning where appropriate. The novice should be instructed in the correct pronunciation and use of the name within the monastic community.

#### **18. Closing the Ceremony**

Once the candidate has received the robes, undertaken the Three Refuges and Ten Training Precepts, established the teacher--student relationship, and received the necessary instructions, the formal ordination gathering may be brought to a close according to the customs of the Order.

The novice pays homage to the preceptor and the assembled Saṅgha as directed.

The preceptor offers a final exhortation, reminding the novice that the ceremony marks the beginning of training rather than its completion.

The novice should leave the ceremony with a clear understanding of the commitments undertaken and the daily discipline expected of a renunciant.

### Part V. Life and Training After Ordination

#### **19. Initial Instruction in Monastic Life**

Following ordination, the novice begins training under the guidance of the Upajjhāya and Ācariya.

The first period of training should establish a sound foundation in the daily conduct and spiritual disciplines of novice life. Instruction should include:

- The meaning and observance of the Ten Training Precepts.

- Proper conduct toward the teachers, fellow monastics, and lay supporters.

- The appropriate use and care of robes, the alms bowl, and other requisites.

- The principles of simplicity, contentment, sense restraint, and dependence on generosity.

- The daily schedule of the monastery or training centre.

- The study and recollection of essential Dhamma teachings.

- The practice of meditation and the development of concentration.

- The cultivation of wisdom through careful observation and investigation of experience.

The novice should have opportunities to ask questions and clarify matters of conduct. Guidance should be given consistently, with attention to both the outward discipline of monastic life and the inward development of the mind.

#### **20. Training in sīla, samādhi, and paññā**

The Ten Training Precepts establish the ethical foundation of novice life. Their purpose is not limited to avoiding misconduct; they also support the gradual transformation of one\'s habits, intentions, and relationship with the world. Within the Arahanist path, training proceeds through the development of virtue (*sīla*), concentration (*samādhi*), and wisdom (*paññā*).

**Virtue (*Sīla*)** establishes restraint and reduces the disturbances created by unwholesome bodily and verbal actions. It supports trust, simplicity, and a mind less burdened by remorse.

**Concentration (*Samādhi*)** is developed through systematic meditation, the cultivation of collectedness, and the gradual calming of mental agitation. The novice learns to direct attention inward rather than continually seeking stimulation and satisfaction from external conditions.

**Wisdom (*Paññā*)** develops through the investigation of experience, including the nature of the body, feelings, perceptions, mental formations, and consciousness. Through sustained practice, the novice learns to recognize impermanence, the unsatisfactory nature of conditioned existence, and the absence of lasting refuge in worldly phenomena.

These dimensions of training support one another. Virtue provides the foundation for concentration; concentration supports clear investigation; and wisdom deepens disenchantment with craving and attachment. The novice should be taught to regard ordination not as an isolated event but as the beginning of a sustained course of spiritual cultivation.

#### **21. The Purpose of Renunciation**

The novice life is directed toward liberation from the defilements that bind the mind to worldly existence.

Renunciation therefore requires more than external simplicity. It calls for a gradual reduction of craving, aversion, and delusion through disciplined conduct, meditation, and wisdom.

The novice should learn to examine the motives behind ordinary habits and desires, including the search for sensory pleasure, possessions, status, entertainment, and security through material conditions.

By simplifying external life and cultivating inner composure, the renunciant creates favourable conditions for the development of concentration and insight.

The preceptor should encourage steady progress without treating spiritual development as a matter of mere appearance, titles, or ceremonial status. The measure of training lies in the gradual transformation of conduct and mind.

Within Arahanism, the ultimate direction of this training is Arahantship: the complete eradication of the defilements and liberation from dukkha.

#### **22. Continuing Guidance and Review**

The preceptor and teacher should continue to guide the novice after the ordination ceremony.

They should provide regular opportunities to review the novice\'s understanding of the precepts, daily conduct, meditation practice, and progress in study.

When difficulties arise, the novice should be encouraged to approach the preceptor honestly and seek instruction. The purpose of such guidance is to strengthen understanding, correct errors, and sustain the training.

The novice, in turn, should cultivate respect for instruction, willingness to learn, and diligence in practice.

Progress should be assessed through the consistency of the novice\'s conduct, the sincerity of the commitment to renunciation, and the development of the qualities required for deeper spiritual practice.

### Part VI. Ceremonial Preparation Checklist

The following checklist is intended to help the preceptor and assisting monastics prepare for the ceremony without interrupting its spiritual atmosphere.

**Before the Ordination**

- The candidate has received instruction on the meaning and responsibilities of Pabbajjā.

- The candidate\'s readiness and relevant eligibility requirements have been reviewed.

- Necessary family, legal, financial, and personal responsibilities have been addressed.

- The ordination date and place have been confirmed.

- The preceptor and any assisting monastics have been confirmed.

- The robes and alms bowl have been prepared.

- The offering tray and other necessary requisites are ready.

- The candidate has prepared clean white clothing.

- The ceremonial space has been arranged.

- The Pāli recitations are available and have been checked.

- Arrangements have been made for the novice\'s initial training.

**During the Ceremony**

- The candidate enters and pays homage.

- The candidate requests Pabbajjā.

- The preceptor gives the initial instruction on refuge and renunciation.

- The candidate receives instruction in the Taca Pañcaka Kammaṭṭhāna.

- The preceptor presents the robes.

- The candidate dons the robes and recites the reflection on their use.

- The candidate requests the Three Refuges and Ten Training Precepts.

- The candidate undertakes the Three Refuges.

- The candidate undertakes the Ten Training Precepts.

- The preceptor gives the admonition on maintaining the training.

- The candidate requests a preceptor and, where applicable, a separate teacher.

- The novice receives the alms bowl and Pāli name.

- The preceptor gives the final instructions.

**After the Ceremony**

- The novice understands the basic expectations of monastic life.

- The novice knows whom to approach for instruction and guidance.

- The initial daily schedule and training arrangements have been explained.

- The novice has received guidance on the Ten Training Precepts.

- The next steps in Dhamma study and meditation training have been established.

**Concluding Reflection**

Pabbajjā is the beginning of a life directed toward liberation. The robes, the Three Refuges, the Ten Training Precepts, and the relationship with the preceptor establish the outward foundation of renunciation. Their deeper purpose is fulfilled through the novice\'s continued practice.

The novice should therefore approach this undertaking with sincerity, humility, and diligence. Each day offers an opportunity to strengthen virtue, deepen concentration, develop wisdom, and loosen the attachments that sustain dukkha.

Through sustained training under the guidance of experienced monastics, the Sāmaṇera or Sāmaṇerī begins the journey of renunciation with a clear direction: the cultivation of the path leading toward Arahantship.

**May this ordination establish a firm foundation for the practice of renunciation, the development of the Noble Path, and the realization of liberation.**

## UPASAMPADĀ: Full Bhikkhu • Bhikkhunī Ordination & Pāṭimokkha

**Arahanist Order of Canada**

**Introduction: The Meaning of Upasampadā**

**Upasampadā** is the formal ordination through which a qualified candidate enters the complete discipline of Bhikkhu or Bhikkhunī life. Following **Pabbajjā**, which establishes the beginning of novice renunciation, **Upasampadā** marks a further commitment to the monastic life governed by the **Bhikkhu / Bhikkhunī** Vinaya.

Within the Arahanist Order of Canada, full ordination is understood as a solemn undertaking directed toward Arahantship. The candidate accepts the responsibilities of fully ordained monastic life and commits to the systematic cultivation of *sīla*, *samādhi*, and *paññā*.

The robes and formal status of a Bhikkhu or Bhikkhunī are not ends in themselves. They establish the **Path** (***Maggo***) for a life of renunciation, sense restraint, meditation, study, and the gradual eradication of the defilements that sustain bondage to worldly existence.

Full ordination therefore entails responsibilities toward Gautama's Dhamma, the Vinaya, the Saṅgha, one\'s teachers, fellow monastics, and the lay community that supports the renunciant life.

These guidelines establish the principles governing preparation for Upasampadā, the responsibilities undertaken following ordination, and the continuing observance of the Vinaya and Pāṭimokkha within the Arahanist Order of Canada.

### Part I. The Foundation of Full Ordination

#### **1. The Purpose of Upasampadā**

Upasampadā establishes the candidate\'s formal entry into the discipline of a fully ordained monastic. It carries greater responsibilities than novice ordination and requires a thorough understanding of the obligations associated with the Bhikkhu or Bhikkhunī life.

The candidate should understand that full ordination entails:

- Acceptance of the applicable Vinaya discipline.

- Observance of the Pāṭimokkha rules governing the relevant monastic community.

- Participation in the formal communal observances required by the applicable Vinaya.

- Training under a qualified teacher in accordance with the traditional requirements of *nissaya*.

- Systematic study of the Dhamma and Vinaya.

- Sustained cultivation of samatha and vipassanā.

- A commitment to simplicity, sense restraint, contentment, and the relinquishment of worldly attachments.

- Conduct that protects the integrity of the Saṅgha and maintains the confidence of the lay community.

The candidate should approach Upasampadā with a clear understanding that full ordination is the beginning of a deeper course of training, not the completion of spiritual development.

#### **2. Eligibility and Readiness**

Before an ordination date is confirmed, the preceptor and the responsible monastics must establish that the candidate satisfies the eligibility requirements of the applicable Vinaya tradition.

The assessment should address the following matters:

1.  **Completion of novice training:** The candidate has completed the required period of Sāmaṇera or Sāmaṇerī training, subject to the Order\'s established policies and any applicable exceptions.

2.  **Knowledge of monastic discipline:** The candidate demonstrates an appropriate understanding of the responsibilities of full ordination and is prepared to undertake the relevant training rules.

3.  **Personal readiness:** The candidate has a stable intention to continue the renunciant life and understands the responsibilities associated with full ordination.

4.  **Eligibility under the Vinaya:** The candidate satisfies the applicable requirements concerning age, consent, prior ordination, health-related and other specified eligibility matters, and any required permissions.

5.  **Guidance and preparation:** A qualified preceptor and the necessary assisting monastics are available to conduct the procedure in accordance with the applicable Vinaya.

The candidate should have opportunities to ask questions and receive clear instruction before the ceremony. The preceptor must not treat ordination as a merely ceremonial appointment; the candidate must understand the nature of the undertaking.

The formal requirements for Bhikkhu and Bhikkhunī ordination are not identical in every respect. Each ordination must therefore follow the procedures and qualifications applicable to the relevant Saṅgha and Vinaya tradition.

#### **3. The Relationship Between Pabbajjā and Upasampadā**

Pabbajjā and Upasampadā are distinct stages of monastic formation.

Pabbajjā establishes novice renunciation. The novice undertakes the Ten Training Precepts and begins systematic training in monastic conduct, meditation, and the Dhamma.

Upasampadā establishes full monastic status under the applicable Vinaya. The fully ordained monastic undertakes the more extensive discipline governing Bhikkhu or Bhikkhunī life.

Within the Arahanist Order of Canada, the ordinary formation pathway provides sustained training at each stage before progression to the next. Completion of a specified period of training does not automatically qualify a candidate for full ordination; readiness and eligibility must be assessed according to the applicable requirements.

### Part II. The Upasampadā Ceremony

#### **4. Governing Principles**

The Upasampadā ceremony must be conducted in accordance with the applicable Vinaya procedure. Its validity depends on fulfilling the relevant requirements, including the qualifications of the participating monastics, the proper constitution of the Saṅgha, the prescribed formal acts, and the candidate\'s eligibility.

The Arahanist Order of Canada should not substitute a newly composed ceremony for the formal procedure required by the Vinaya tradition under which the ordination is conducted.

The following principles govern preparation:

- The candidate must receive prior formal training concerning the purpose and consequences of full ordination.

- The preceptor must confirm the candidate\'s eligibility.

- The required number and qualifications of participating monastics must be established according to the applicable Vinaya.

- The formal act of the Saṅgha must be conducted according to the prescribed procedure.

- Any required questions, declarations, motions, and announcements must follow the relevant Vinaya tradition.

- The ordination must be recorded accurately in the Order\'s institutional records.
