In the first three approaches, samatha and vipassanā are developed in different sequences or in conjunction. In the fourth, the sutta explicitly describes the citta becoming established in Higher Self / Soul-Spirit, unified, and concentrated.

Samādhi is not simply the absence of thought. Nor is it merely a temporary feeling of relaxation.

It refers to the concentration and unification of the mind, developed through sustained training.

An untrained mind is easily dispersed. Attention moves from one object to another, and the practitioner repeatedly becomes absorbed in changing circumstances. Even when the body is physically still, the mind may remain occupied with desire, aversion, planning, memories, and imagined possibilities.

The cultivation of samādhi addresses this lack of continuity.

As the practitioner develops concentration, the mind becomes increasingly capable of remaining with its meditation object without being repeatedly pulled away. Its attention becomes more stable, and meditation becomes deeper and more sustained.

Depending on the practitioner\'s development and the method employed, concentration may progress through increasingly refined degrees of unification.

The traditional distinction between access concentration and absorption concentration can be useful in guiding this development. Access concentration refers to a developed degree of collectedness that approaches absorption, while absorption involves a deeper unification of mind associated with the jhānas.

These states require systematic cultivation and should not be assumed simply because a practitioner experiences calmness, unusual sensations, or temporary happiness.

**The Importance of Mental Unification for Liberation**

The immediate benefit of samādhi is stability, but its significance for Arahanism lies in its relationship to the liberation path.

A concentrated mind is better able to sustain investigation without being continually distracted. It can observe mental processes more clearly and remain collected while examining experiences that would otherwise provoke strong attraction or aversion.

Samādhi also allows the practitioner to experience forms of happiness and contentment that do not depend in the ordinary way on the acquisition of external objects.

Such experiences can help demonstrate the limitations of worldly gratification. The practitioner learns through practice that the mind can be cultivated toward profound inner happiness rather than continually seeking satisfaction through sensory stimulation.

**Samādhi provides the concentration needed for the work of liberation from worldly bondage; it does not make the work of liberation unnecessary.**

The objective is to cultivate concentration as part of the path toward the eradication of the underlying tendencies that bind the practitioner to worldly existence.

### 7. Vipassanā: From Observing Experience to Abandoning Worldly Bondage

Vipassanā is often introduced through the observation of bodily sensations, feelings, thoughts, and other mental phenomena. These are important fields of investigation, but the purpose of vipassanā in our training must be understood in relation to the ultimate objective of Arahanism.

We cultivate vipassanā because the mind must understand the nature of its involvement with the world and develop the liberating knowledge through which worldly attachment and ignorance are overcome.

The practice therefore requires more than passive observation.

The practitioner must learn to distinguish between the occurrence of an experience and the desire, aversion, or confusion that develops in relation to it.

A pleasant feeling may arise naturally. Worldly bondage develops through the attraction, appropriation, and craving for external objects that may follow it.

An unpleasant feeling may arise naturally. Worldly bondage is reinforced when the mind responds with resistance, hostility, or an insistent demand that experience conform to its preferences.

A neutral experience may appear insignificant. Yet the mind can remain bound through ignorance when it fails to recognize what is occurring and continues to operate through habitual patterns.

Vipassanā investigates these relationships.

**Observing the Body**

The body is an important foundation for meditation because bodily experience provides a continuous field in which attention can be developed.

The practitioner observes posture, breathing, movement, physical sensations, and the changing conditions of bodily experience.

The purpose is not to become preoccupied with the body but to understand it through direct investigation.

The body changes. Its condition depends on multiple causes and circumstances. It experiences comfort and discomfort, strength and weakness, and continual physical processes beyond complete voluntary control.

Through sustained contemplation, the practitioner can begin to distinguish direct bodily experience from the additional layers of attraction, aversion, and identification that the mind constructs around it.

This investigation supports renunciation by weakening the tendency to make bodily gratification the central purpose of life.

**Observing Feelings**

Feelings are particularly important because they influence the movement of craving and aversion.

The practitioner learns to recognize pleasant, unpleasant, and neutral feeling without immediately following the habitual response associated with each.

When pleasant feeling arises, the task is to understand it rather than automatically demand its continuation.

When unpleasant feeling arises, the task is to understand it rather than automatically resist it.

When neutral feeling arises, the task is to remain discerning rather than lapse into unawareness.

The practitioner gradually develops a clearer understanding of how feelings condition subsequent reactions.

This does not mean suppressing all feeling or becoming indifferent to the welfare of others. It means cultivating the freedom to respond without being governed by compulsive attraction or aversion.

**Observing Mental States**

The practitioner also investigates the condition of the mind itself.

Is the mind agitated or tranquil? Is it dominated by desire, aversion, confusion, restlessness, or collectedness? Is attention being pulled toward external objects, or is the citta becoming stable?

Such investigation helps the practitioner recognize the difference between a temporary state and an underlying tendency.

A period of calm does not necessarily mean that the causes of agitation have been eradicated. Likewise, the appearance of a disturbing thought does not by itself establish that the practitioner has failed in meditation.

What matters is the development of understanding and the progressive weakening of the tendencies that repeatedly condition the mind.

**Investigating Worldly Craving, Aversion, and Delusion**

The deepest purpose of vipassanā is to understand the processes that maintain bondage.

The practitioner examines how worldly attraction becomes craving, how craving generates repeated mental involvement, how aversion produces resistance, and how delusion prevents the mind from understanding its own condition.

This investigation must eventually extend beyond individual thoughts and emotions to the underlying patterns that sustain them.

The practitioner is not merely trying to become less angry, less anxious, or less distracted in daily life. These improvements are valuable, but the ultimate objective is more radical: the eradication of the roots of worldly bondage.

Vipassanā therefore supports the transition from temporary control of mental states to the deeper work of liberation.

### 8. Samatha and Vipassanā Within the Discipline of Renunciation

Meditation does not develop in isolation from the way a practitioner lives.

The mind is conditioned by habits, activities, relationships, sensory stimulation, speech, and conduct. If these influences remain unexamined, they can continually reinforce the very patterns that meditation is intended to weaken.

For this reason, our training places meditation within a framework of sense restraint, ethical discipline and renunciation.

The monastic life provides a structured environment in which the practitioner can withdraw from many ordinary demands and devote sustained attention to the cultivation of the mind.

Renunciation is not merely a change of clothing or an external declaration of spiritual aspiration. It is a progressive reorientation of life away from dependence on worldly gratification and toward the development of liberation from worldly bondage.

**Sīla: The Foundation of the Training**

Sīla, or ethical discipline, establishes the conditions in which meditation can develop.

When conduct is repeatedly governed by dishonesty, hostility, sensual indulgence, or the pursuit of personal advantage, the mind becomes accustomed to patterns that disturb concentration and reinforce attachment.

Ethical discipline works in the opposite direction. It restrains harmful conduct, reduces avoidable conflict, and helps establish a life consistent with the objective of liberation from worldly bondage.

For those beginning the path, the Five Precepts provide an initial ethical foundation. More intensive training may involve the Eight Precepts, while those undertaking renunciant training progress toward the discipline appropriate to their stage of ordination.

In the monastic context, the discipline of a bhikkhu or bhikkhunī extends far beyond a short list of ethical rules. It governs conduct, relationships, possessions, daily routines, and the responsibilities of monastic life.

The purpose of this discipline is not simply to create an orderly institution. It is to support the transformation of the practitioner\'s habits and orientation.

**Renunciation and the Reduction of Distraction**

Every form of renunciation reduces some of the demands that ordinary worldly life places upon the mind.

A person who continually pursues entertainment, possessions, status, sensual gratification, and social recognition must devote considerable attention to maintaining these activities. The mind becomes accustomed to seeking stimulation and responding to an expanding range of externally worldly desires.

A renunciant gradually relinquishes this pattern.

By simplifying possessions, reducing unnecessary activity, observing ethical discipline, sense restraint and organizing life around meditation and Dhamma practice, the practitioner creates conditions more conducive to concentration, samādhi.

This does not mean that renunciation automatically produces samādhi or guarantees vipassanā. The mind can carry its attachments into a monastery just as it can carry them into ordinary life.

Renunciation provides the conditions for training; the practitioner must still undertake the training itself.

**Brahmacariya: A Life Directed Toward Liberation from Worldly Bondage**

Brahmacariya, the holy life, expresses the comprehensive orientation of renunciant practice.

It involves more than temporary abstention from particular activities. It is a disciplined way of life in which conduct, relationships, time, and personal priorities are organized around the path.

In our training, the monastic ideal is central because Arahantship is the objective toward which the entire discipline is directed.

A practitioner undertaking this training learns to regard meditation not as an occasional activity added to an otherwise unchanged life, but as an essential part of a broader transformation.

The day is organized to support mindfulness, concentration, contemplation, instruction, and the observance of discipline. Periods of silence and reduced stimulation allow the mind to settle. Regular routines reduce unnecessary decision-making. Guidance from an experienced teacher helps the practitioner understand difficulties and maintain an appropriate direction.

Through this integration, renunciation becomes a practical foundation for the cultivation of samatha, and vipassanā.

### 9. The Relationship Between Inner Happiness and Renunciation

An important aspect of the training is the development of inner happiness and joy.

Ordinary worldly life often encourages the belief that happiness must be obtained through external conditions: desirable objects, sensory pleasures, recognition, possessions, relationships, or the fulfillment of personal ambitions.

The pursuit of these things can become repetitive. A desired experience is obtained, its satisfaction changes, and the mind begins searching for something else.

Even when circumstances are favorable, the mind may remain restless because its happiness depends on conditions that cannot be permanently secured.

Meditation offers a different direction.

Through ethical discipline, sense restraint, renunciation, and the development of samādhi, the practitioner cultivates forms of contentment, happiness, and joy that arise through the training of the mind itself.

The Theragāthā and Therīgāthā preserve the voices of early Elder Bhikkhus and Bhikkhunis who describe the satisfaction and freedom associated with the renunciant life. Their verses remind us that withdrawal from worldly pursuits need not be understood merely as deprivation. It can also be a positive movement toward a different source of happiness.

For our retreat, this is an important principle.

Renunciation should not be approached solely as the suppression of desire or the endurance of hardship. It must be accompanied by the cultivation of the qualities that make the renunciant life spiritually fruitful.

As the mind becomes more concentrated, it can discover a degree of contentment that does not require constant sensory gratification. As worldly attachment weakens, the practitioner becomes less dependent on the acquisition and preservation of external worldly conditions.

Inner happiness can therefore support renunciation by reducing the perceived necessity of worldly gratification.

Nevertheless, meditative happiness must be understood within the complete path. The practitioner should neither reject wholesome inner joy nor mistake it for the final objective.

The training must therefore combine the cultivation of inner happiness with the vipassanā necessary to transcend worldly attachment itself.

**Inner happiness supports renunciation; liberation from worldly bondage requires the abandonment of the bondage that persists even when happiness is present.**

### 10. The Abandonment of Fetters and Underlying Tendencies

The repeated conclusion of AN 4.170 is the most important criterion for understanding the four approaches.

In every case, the practitioner pursues, develops, and cultivates the path. As this development continues, the fetters are abandoned and the underlying tendencies are eliminated.

The sutta does not conclude by praising a particular meditation technique or describing an isolated experience of concentration. It directs attention toward the transformation accomplished through the path.

This distinction is essential.

A practitioner may become proficient in sitting meditation without abandoning the underlying attachment to worldly existence. Another may experience powerful concentration while remaining vulnerable to desire or aversion. A third may develop considerable knowledge of the Dhamma while continuing to be governed by habitual patterns.

The development of these abilities may be valuable, but they must not be confused with the complete eradication of worldly bondage.

**The Fetters and Underlying Tendencies**

The fetters (*saṁyojanāni*) are the bonds that keep beings attached to worldly existence. The underlying tendencies (*anusayā*) refer to deeply rooted dispositions that can continue to influence the mind even when they are not immediately manifest.

A person may refrain from acting out of anger while the underlying tendency toward aversion remains. One may avoid pursuing a particular desire while the deeper inclination toward sensual gratification remains. One may understand a teaching intellectually while the underlying tendency toward ignorance persists.

This is why the distinction between temporary restraint and complete eradication matters.

Ethical discipline can restrain harmful conduct. Concentration can suppress or temporarily quiet certain mental disturbances. Vipassanā can reveal their causes and weaken their influence.

The path to Arahantship must culminate in the complete eradication of the relevant fetters and underlying tendencies, not merely their temporary absence.

**Worldly Bondage in the Pali Canon**

The concepts of bondage and being bonded means the deep psychological and cosmological mechanisms that bind the **Citta** (mind-heart) to **saṃsāric worldly existence (bhāva)** and the world (**loka**). The state of being bonded is defined as being trapped in involuntary reincarnation, subject to birth, aging, sickness, and death.

**(1). The Primary Classifications of Bondage**

The Pali Canon categorizes bondage under several precise technical frameworks:

**A. The Ten Fetters (*Samyojana*)**

The *Samyojanas* are the primary bonds that tie an untrained worldly person (*puthujjana*) to the wheel of rebirth (*samsāra*). They are divided into two sets:

**Five Lower Fetters (*Orambhāgiya-saṁyojana*)** --- Bind the Citta to the lower realms of existence (sensual sphere):

> **Sakkāya-diṭṭhi:** Real/True (**Sa**) Body (**kāya**) view (**diṭṭhi**) (believing the body is true, or ultimately real).
>
> **Vicikicchā:** Skeptical doubt regarding the ultimate deathless reality and the validity for the ancient path of liberation from the world.
>
> **Sīlabbata-parāmāsa:** Wrong, misapprehension, blind attachment to (**parāmāsa**) the vow / observance (**bata/vata**) of **Sīla**: merit hunter or overly fear for observance-failures, abused by the Jataka stories beyond moral teaching, not knowing Sīla is purely a training place for gradual withdrawal from the world.
>
> **Kāma-chanda:** Sexual and sensual desire / craving for its stimulants.
>
> **Byāpāda:** fear, anger, Ill-will, resentment, aversion and hate (*dosa*).

**Five Higher Fetters (*Uddhambhāgiya-saṁyojana*)** --- Bind the Citta to the higher rupa and arupa realms:

> **Rūparāga:** attachment to *rūpa* realm (*rūpa-dhatu*).
>
> **Arūparāga:** attachment to *arūpa* realm (*arūpa-dhatu*).
>
> **Māna:** Ego, conceit, worldly pride.
>
> **Uddhacca:** Restlessness, mental agitation.
>
> **Avijjā:** Ignorance of the three knowledges (**tisso** **vijjā**): knowing what reality is (cosmology), knowing how the universe operates across the history of the cosmic mankind race---including the Brahma source---and knowing how these processes are brought to final resolution or completion: that is, liberation from worldly entrapment, specifically for the mankind race, into the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

**B. The Three Root Fires / Poisonous Bonds:** At the core of all bondage to the world lie the three roots of defilements:

- **Rāga:** Sexual craving, lust, passion, worldly attachment.

- **Dosa:** Fear, anger, violence, hatred, ill-will.

- **Moha:** Ignorance and delusion, not knowing the path to liberation.

**C. The Four Bondages / Ties (*Yoga* & *Gantha*)**

The Suttas often use the word **Yoga** (literally \"yoke\" or \"bond\") and **Kāyaganthi** (\"bodily tie/knot\"):

a.  **Kāmayoga:** The bond of sexuality and sensuality.

b.  **Bhavayoga:** The bond of worldly existence/becoming.

c.  **Diṭṭhiyoga:** The bond of wrong views and distorted understanding: such as the wrong-awakening to non-dual knowledge or non-dual reality, which leads to dissolution back into the source of the universe; and the wrong understanding of *Anattā* as \"no self\" or \"no soul,\" or the reductionist view that non-self implies the five aggregates constitute the entirety of human existence.

d.  **Avijjāyoga:** The bond of ignorance, not knowing the three knowledges: not knowing the cosmology, true nature or laws of the world. Not knowing the conflict between the civilizational laws and values of cosmic mankind race (from Brahma), and the natural way of the universal laws (from the source of universe). Not knowing what it truly means to be liberated from the world and universe.

**(2). The State of Being Bonded (*Baddha*)**

In the Pali Suttas, an individual who is bonded (*baddha*) is described through vivid structural and existential characteristics:

**I. Subjection to Māra's Snares (*Māra-pāsa*)**

**Māra** is the **personification** of **Death**, the universal law of decay and dissolution. The ancient Vedic metaphor of Varuṇa's noose (*pāśa*) evolves in the Theravada Pali Suttas into **Māra's snare** (*Māra-pāsa*). Anyone bound by *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* is caught in Māra\'s net: *Kāmesu giddhā pasutā pamuttā, Anayaṃ samāpannā na hi jānamānā; Nandirāgabaddhā mārapāsena baddhā, Na te pamuccanti mārabandhanā.*

Greedy for sexual and sensual pleasures (kāmesu giddhā), intent upon (pasutā) and immersed in them (pamuttā), Fallen into ruin (anayaṃ samāpannā), truly without knowing (na hi jānamānā); Bound by delight in worldly craving and attachment (nandi-rāga-baddhā), bound (baddhā) by Māra\'s snare (māra-pāsena), They are not (na te) released (pamuccanti) from Māra\'s fetter (māra-bandhanā). (Sutta Nipāta 4.13)

**II. Misapprehension of *Anattā***

The state of worldly bondage is fundamentally perpetuated by a double-sided misunderstanding of *Anattā*, further amplified in the modern era by secular, psychological mistranslations:

a)  **The Modern Translational Distortion (\"No-Self\" as Psychological Reductionism):**

Under the influence of 20th-century secular academia and Western psychology, the Pāḷi term *attā* has been flattened into the abstract psychological concept of \"self\" or \"ego identity.\" This mistranslation distorts *Anattā* into the secular doctrine of \"no-self,\" misleading seekers into believing that ancient spiritual realization is merely a psychological theory claiming human beings possess no soul-spirit (*attā*) or spiritual essence---reducing the entirety of human existence to a temporary, mechanical cluster of five physical and cognitive aggregates (*pañcakkhandha*) that completely disintegrates upon physical death.

b)  **The Materialist/Annihilationist Fallacy (*Ucchedadiṭṭhi*):**

Building upon this translational mistake, this reductionist view assumes that because the five aggregates are *Anattā*, there is no transcendent spiritual entity whatsoever. To justify this claim, proponents of secular reductionism attempt to anchor their \"no-soul\" theory in the teaching of Conditioned Dependability (*Paṭiccasamuppāda*). They argue that human existence is nothing more than a closed, self-contained loop of interdependent mental and physical phenomena---an empty stream of cause and effect where \"impersonal processes give rise to impersonal processes,\" with no underlying soul-spirit (*attā*) or enduring essence behind them. From this premise, they assert that liberation is simply the mechanical halting of this continuous chain of becoming, treating ultimate liberation as total annihilation (*ucchedavāda*)---the exact materialist error explicitly rejected throughout the Pāḷi Canon.

**The True Meaning of Dependent Origination (*Paṭiccasamuppāda*)**

This reductionist interpretation fundamentally misconstrues the purpose and scope of Dependent Origination:

i.  **A Law of the World (*Loka*), Not the Boundless Citta:**

*Paṭiccasamuppāda* does not describe the absolute nature of reality or the totality of existence. Rather, it specifically maps the causal architecture of **worldly bondage, friction, and involuntary reincarnation** (*dukkha*). It demonstrates precisely how the Citta, when blinded by ignorance (*avijjā*), becomes entangled in the worldly apparatus of cause and effect through worldly craving (*taṇhā*) and worldly clinging (*upādāna*).

ii. **Diagnosing the Entrapment Mechanism:**

The twelve links of *Paṭiccasamuppāda* are not an ontological proof that \"nothing exists except conditions.\" Instead, they serve as a diagnostic blueprint showing how the Citta generates worldly becoming (*bhava*) and worldly birth (*jāti*). It shows that as long as the Citta identifies with conditioned phenomena (by the universal law of decay and dissolution), it remains trapped in this samsāric worldly existence.

iii. **Conditioned Cessation vs. Absolute Annihilation:**

When the teaching speaks of the cessation of Dependent Origination (*avijjāyatveva asesavirāganirodhā saṅkhāranirodho\...*), it does not mean the annihilation of a living being or the destruction of the soul-spirit. It signifies the **complete collapse of the worldly causal chain**.

By shattering ignorance through Paññā and burning out latent passion kamma seeds through the primordial force of *tapas*, the Citta severs the fuel of worldly becoming. It drops the five aggregates---recognizing them as worldly burdens belonging strictly to the laws of cause and effect of the world (*loka*)---withdraws its interest, leaves worldly phenomena behind, and transcends them. Rather than dissolving into non-existence, the purified, untrammeled Citta is freed from the closed circuit of conditionality altogether, realizing its true, unshakeable sanctuary in the deathless realm of *Nibbāna-Dhātu*.

c)  **The Pseudo-Monistic/Non-Dual Entrapment:**

On the opposite extreme, pseudo-awakening to a non-dual view mistakes the non-duality for the divine, leading to dissolution back into the conscious source of the universe rather than true transcendence out of *saṁsāra*.

To truly understand *Anattā* according to the early Pāḷi Canon is not to proclaim the secular slogan \"there is no soul.\" Rather, *Anattā* serves as a rigorous analytical tool of **disenchantment and dis-identification**. It explicitly targets the five world-bound aggregates (*rūpa, vedanā, saññā, saṅkhārā, viññāṇa*) to declare:

*\"This is not mine; I am not this; this is not my soul-spirit.\"*

The fundamental error lies in mistaking the *objects being discarded* (the five aggregates) for the *totality of what exists*. Those bound by this view fail to comprehend that the true spiritual essence (*attā*)---the purified, untrammeled Citta---is entirely beyond, independent of, and uncontained by the five worldly aggregates.

**Textual Proofs & Citations from the Pāḷi Canon**

**The Triad Formula of Dis-identification (*Anattalakkhaṇa Sutta*, SN 22.59 / Vin I 13):** In this sutta, *Anattā* is defined not as a categorical denial of spiritual reality, but as a systematic methodology to dis-identify from conditioned, worldly phenomena: *\"Rūpaṁ\... vedanā\... saññā\... saṅkhārā\... viññāṇaṁ anattā. Taṁ kiṁ maññatha, bhikkhave, rūpaṁ niccaṁ vā aniccaṁ vā\'ti? --- Aniccaṁ, bhante. Yaṁ panāniccaṁ dukkhaṁ vā taṁ sukhaṁ vā\'ti? --- Dukkhaṁ, bhante. Yaṁ panāniccaṁ dukkhaṁ vipariṇāmadhammaṁ, kallaṁ nu taṁ samanupassituṁ: \'Etaṁ mama, eso\'hamasmi, eso me attā\'ti? --- No hetaṁ, bhante.*

*"Form\... feeling\... perception\... mental formations\... consciousness is not the soul-spirit (anattā). What do you think, monks, is form permanent or impermanent?" --- "Impermanent, Venerable Sir." --- "Is that which is impermanent suffering or happiness?" --- "Suffering, Venerable Sir." --- "Is it fitting then to regard that which is impermanent, suffering, and subject to change as: \'This is mine, this I am, this is my soul-spirit\'?" --- "Definitely not, Venerable Sir."*

**Analysis:** The discourse does not state \"there is no soul-spirit.\" It rigorously instructs the seeker to examine each of the five aggregates, recognize their impermanent (*anicca*) and unsatisfying (*dukkha*) nature, and systematically reject them as \"not my soul-spirit\" (*na me so attā*).

**The Five Aggregates Are Not the Whole Being (*Yamaka Sutta*, SN 22.85):** In SN 22.85, bhikkhu Yamaka holds the harmful, reductionist view (*pāpakaṁ diṭṭhigataṁ*) that a liberated being is completely annihilated at death because human existence consists *only* of the five aggregates that break apart. Venerable Sāriputta corrects this exact misunderstanding: *\"Taṁ kiṁ maññasi, āvuso yamaka, rūpaṁ tathāgato\'ti? --- No hetaṁ, āvuso. Vedanā\... Saññā\... Saṅkhārā\... Viññāṇaṁ tathāgato\'ti? --- No hetaṁ, āvuso. Aññatra rūpā\... vedanāya\... saññāya\... saṅkhārehi\... viññāṇā tathāgato\'ti? --- No hetaṁ, āvuso.*

*"What do you think, friend Yamaka: Do you regard physical form as the Tathāgata?" --- "No, friend." --- "Do you regard feeling\... perception\... choices\... consciousness as the Tathāgata?" --- "No, friend." --- "Do you regard the Tathāgata as existing apart from form, feeling, perception, choices, or consciousness?" --- "No, friend." --- "Since the Tathāgata cannot be pinned down or trapped as real and true even here in this very life within the five aggregates, is it proper for you to declare that a realized being is annihilated upon death?"*

**Analysis:** Sāriputta proves that equating a liberated being (*Tathāgata,* literally means having resolved to go there) strictly with the five aggregates---or assuming that nothing remains once the aggregates dissolve---is a fundamental delusion born of materialist reductionism.

**Rejection of Both \"No Soul-Spirit\" and \"Soul-Spirit is the Aggregates\" (*Sabbāsava Sutta*, MN 2):** In MN 2, the Buddha explicitly classifies the dogmatic assertion \"I have no soul-spirit\" (*natthi me attā*) as a thicket of views (*diṭṭhigahana*) and a fetter (*diṭṭhibandhana*) that locks a person into worldly bondage: *\"Tassa evaṁ ayoniso manasikaroto channaṁ diṭṭhīnaṁ aññatarā diṭṭhi uppajjati: \'Atthi me attā\'ti vā assa saccato thetato diṭṭhi uppajjati; \'Natthi me attā\'ti vā assa saccato thetato diṭṭhi uppajjati\... Ayaṁ vuccatābhikkhave, diṭṭhigahaṇaṁ diṭṭhikantāraṁ\... diṭṭhisaṁyojana saṁyutto.\"*

*"When one attends unwisely in this way, one of six views arises as true and established: The view \'I have a soul-spirit\' arises\... or the view \'I have no soul-spirit\' arises\... This is called a thicket of views, a wilderness of views, a contortion of views\... bound by the fetter of views."*

**Analysis:** The canonical text explicitly rejects the annihilationist/ materialist stance (\"I have no soul-spirit / there is no soul\") as an unwise, speculative trap. Denying spiritual reality entirely keeps the mind trapped within the worldly framework.

To uncover why the statement **\"I have a soul-spirit\" (*Atthi me attā*)** is classified as an unwisely attended view (*ayoniso manasikāra*). It boils down to **who the \"I\" is** and **the direction of ownership**.

**The Trap of \"I Have a Soul-Spirit\" (*Atthi me attā*)**

When someone says **\"I have a soul-spirit,\"** the sentence grammatically and conceptually sets up a false subject-object relationship: **The False \"I\":** The \"I\" claiming ownership in this statement is actually the **ego/personality rooted in the physical reality and five aggregates** (*rūpa, vedanā, saññā, saṅkhārā, viññāṇa*). **The False \"Object\":** It treats the true spiritual essence (*attā*) as a secondary possession---a \"thing\" that this worldly body-mind complex *owns* or *possesses* (like saying \"I have a car\" or \"I have a house\").

This is an inverted, upside-down understanding. It places the worldly manifestation in the driver\'s seat and treats the transcendent soul-spirit as an attribute or accessory belonging to the physical person.

**Correct Understanding: The Citta / Soul-Spirit as the True Subject**

To correct this inversion we need to place the true subject back where it belongs: **The True Subject:** **I am the soul-spirit (the purified Citta). The Manifestation / Entrapment:** **I have a temporary manifestation / vehicle in this physical reality (the five aggregates).**

Under this understanding: **The Physical/Mental Equipment is the Burden:** The physical body and cognitive machinery are physical/mental phenomena manifested in this physical world (*loka*). They are subject to cause, effect, decay, and death (*dukkha*). **The Identification Error:** Delusion occurs when the true subject (the Citta) misidentifies with its temporary manifestation, thinking: *\"This physical body/mind is me, this is mine, this is my self.\"* **The Trap:** Thinking \"I (this person) have a soul-spirit\" keeps you grounded in the physical identity. Recognizing \"I am the soul-spirit trapped in/manifesting through this temporary world-bound equipment\" restores the true scale of spiritual reality.

**How the Pāḷi Canon Frames This Correction**

This exact distinction is illustrated in the **Bhāra Sutta (SN 22.22)** through the analogy of the **Burden and the Bearer**: **The Burden (*Bhāra*):** The five aggregates---the physical body, feelings, perceptions, choices, and sensory consciousness---are the worldly burden. **The Bearer (*Bhārahāra*):** The individual spiritual entity (the soul-spirit / Citta) is the bearer carrying the burden.

The error of saying *\"I (the body/mind) have a soul-spirit\"* is equivalent to the **burden claiming it owns the bearer**. The correct realization is recognizing that the bearer (the Citta / soul-spirit) is currently carrying an external, heavy, worldly burden. Through sense restraint, disenchantment, Samadhi, and Paññā, the bearer simply **drops the burden** (*bhāranikkhepanaṁ*), severs all worldly attachment, and realizes ultimate liberation in *Nibbāna-Dhātu*.

**The Analogy of Carrying the Worldly Load (*Bhāra Sutta*, SN 22.22)**

In SN 22.22, the Buddha explicitly separates the five aggregates from the true bearer (*bhārahāra*) who carries and eventually drops them: *\"Bhārā ha pañcakkhandhā, bhārahāro ca puggalo; Bhārādānaṁ dukkhaṁ loke, bhāranikkhepanaṁ sukhaṁ.\"*

*"The five aggregates are indeed the burden (bhāra); the individual bearer (bhārahāra) is the carrier of the burden. Taking up the burden is suffering in the world; laying down the burden is supreme bliss."*

**Analysis:** The five aggregates constitute the worldly burden (*bhāra*). The spiritual essence (the Citta / person) is the bearer (*bhārahāra*). Equating the bearer directly with the burden---or claiming the burden is all that exists---is the precise reductionist mistake that prevents the Citta from dropping the load, achieving dispassion (*virāga*), and realizing ultimate liberation (*Vimutti*) in *Nibbāna-Dhātu*.

**Involuntary Reincarnation (*Saṃsāra*)**

Because of worldly craving (*taṇhā*) and clinging (*upādāna*), the Citta is perpetually yoked to new worldly existence. Upon the dissolution of one body, the Citta is propelled by its own worldly attachments into another womb, another world, and another cycle of birth and death.

**The Nature of Liberation (*Vimutti*)**

Just as bondage is the internal tying of the Citta to the world through rāga (worldly craving and attachment), dosa (fear, anger, aversion, violence, and hate), and moha (ignorance and delusion, not knowing the ancient path to liberation from the world), liberation from the world (Vimutti) is the complete untying and unbinding from the world:

**Cessation of Worldly Clinging:** It is through the complete eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha, that we can escape our involuntary reincarnation and gain true freedom.

**Transcendence of the World (Loka):** We are trapped in the world, resulting in involuntary reincarnation across this world and all worlds within the universe. We call the gravitational pull of the world the defilements of the Citta---namely rāga, dosa, and moha. The goal of our practice is to purify the Citta (sever all worldly bondage). The purified Citta will then become disenchanted with the world (nibbidā), fading off worldly passion (virāga), and gain liberation from the world (vimutta).

**The Exit Threshold:** The exit point from saṃsāric worlds to the deathless realm of Nibbāna is specifically through the Pure Abodes Brahma heavens. Upon the dissolution of the final physical body of an Arahant (a fully purified being), the purified Citta of the Arahant exit this world and the universe to go to the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

**The Unconditioned Realm (*Asaṅkhata-Dhātu*), Deathless (Amata), Pure Wholesomeness (Sugata) with Ultimate Peace (Santi)**

Everything in the world is conditioned (*saṅkhata*) by the universal laws of the universe which contains billions of worlds---including the laws of decay and dissolution. Nibbāna-Dhātu is a realm that is completely unconditioned (*asaṅkhata*) by the laws we observe in this universe because it is outside of the jurisdiction of this universe. That is why mankind in the realm of Nibbāna has no death, they can enjoy permanent blissful life, ultimate Peace (Santi), and without sufferings as we experienced here in this world.

In short, the liberated life in the realm of Nibbāna is not nothingness, annihilation or empty voidness; it is a new life with the perfect freedom. In the Pāli Canon, specifically within the **Asaṅkhata Saṁyutta** (SN 43.1--44), the Buddha delivers a series of short discourses outlining the **epithets, characteristics, and synonyms of Nibbāna**. This definitive canonical list contains **35 characteristics** describing what the realm of Nibbāna is like including:

Asaṅkhata (unconditioned) ananta (infinite) anāsava (taintless, undefiled), Sacca (truth, real) pāra (beyond, the other shore) nipuṇa (perfect) sududdasa (hard to be seen); Ajajjara (un-aging, undecaying) dhuva (permament, constant) apalokita (undisintegrating), Anidassana (non-manifestive) nippapa (free from proliferation/diffuseness) santa (calmed, peace).

Amata (deathless) paṇīta (sublime, excellent) siva (auspicious) khema (secure, safe), Taṇhākkhaya (destruction of craving) acchariya (wondrous, extraordinary) abbhuta (un-precedented, marvellous); Anītika (free from harm/ plague) anītikadhamma (of a nature free from harm/ plague), Nibbāna (unbinding) sugata (pure wholesomeness) desita (shown, pointed out).

Abyābajjha (without oppression, un-afflicted) virāga (dispassion), Suddhi (purity, cleanness) mutti (release, unbinding) anālaya (un-clinging, free from attachment); Dīpa (island) leṇa (cave) tāṇa (guard, protection), saraṇa (refuge, shelter) parāyana (final destination, ultimate goal).

### 11. The Four Approaches as a Practical Framework for Our Training

The four approaches are not merely theoretical classifications. They provide a framework for understanding how meditation training can be adapted to the practitioner.

Our retreat uses this framework to guide instruction and practice.

**For the Practitioner Who Needs Mental Stability**

Some practitioners arrive with little experience of sustained meditation. Their attention is easily distracted, their minds are restless, and they may find it difficult to remain with a meditation object.

For them, the initial emphasis may be on samatha.

They learn to establish a stable posture, maintain attention, recognize distraction, and return patiently to the meditation object. The objective is to develop sufficient concentration for more sustained investigation.

Vipassanā is introduced and developed in relation to their growing stability.

**For the Practitioner Who Relies Heavily on Reflection**

Other practitioners have studied the Dhamma extensively or naturally approach meditation through analysis.

They may understand many principles but struggle to settle the mind.

For these practitioners, the training emphasizes the transition from conceptual investigation to direct meditation. They learn to recognize when further thinking is useful and when it has become an obstacle to concentration.

Samatha is developed as a necessary complement to their existing interest in vipassanā.

**For the Practitioner Developing Both Together**

Some practitioners can maintain a reasonable degree of concentration while investigating experience directly.

For them, samatha and vipassanā can be coordinated throughout the training.

Periods of concentrated meditation support the investigation of mental processes. The understanding developed through investigation helps the practitioner recognize and abandon distractions. Both practices deepen through continued cultivation.

**For the Practitioner to be Fully Established in Their Higher Self / Soul-Spirit**

They need to study the Dhamma intensively first, experience persistent agitation associated with spiritual inquiry and Gautama's Dhamma.

They may be preoccupied with questions about liberation, repeatedly compare teachings, or become frustrated by their inability to resolve every uncertainty.

For them, instruction emphasizes the cultivation of inward stability, the reduction of unnecessary conceptual activity during meditation, and the development of samādhi.

Study and discussion remain available at appropriate times, but the practitioner learns to devote meditation periods to the actual training of the citta.

These approaches are not rigid categories into which every practitioner must permanently fit. A practitioner may move between them as training develops. The appropriate balance can change according to the practitioner\'s experience, concentration, understanding, and difficulties.

The purpose of individualized instruction is to support progress without losing sight of the common objective.

### 12. What to Expect During a Meditation Retreat

A meditation retreat offers an opportunity to experience a more concentrated form of training than is usually possible amid ordinary responsibilities.

The precise schedule and intensity of practice may vary according to the length of the retreat, the experience of the participants, and the guidance provided by the instructor. Nevertheless, the training can be organized around several complementary activities.

**Meditation instruction** introduces the relevant method, explains its purpose, and establishes a clear basis for practice. Participants learn how to approach their meditation object, recognize common obstacles, and adjust their practice appropriately.

**Formal meditation** provides sustained periods for developing samatha, vipassanā, or a coordinated combination of the two. Repeated practice allows participants to recognize habitual patterns of distraction and develop greater continuity of attention.

**Dhamma instruction** explains the principles underlying the training. Participants study the relationship between ethical discipline, concentration, vipassanā, renunciation, and liberation from worldly bondage.

**Contemplation and reflection** allow participants to examine how desire, aversion, and ignorance influence their lives. The purpose is not to become absorbed in abstract thinking but to develop an understanding that supports the practical work of meditation.

**Periods of silence and simplicity** reduce unnecessary stimulation and create opportunities for inward stability. By temporarily setting aside ordinary distractions, participants can observe their habits of attention and their dependence on external activity more clearly.

**Teacher guidance** helps participants understand their experiences without drawing premature conclusions. Meditation can involve unfamiliar sensations, changes in attention, periods of calm, and difficulties with restlessness. These experiences should be approached carefully and interpreted in relation to the purpose and method of practice.

A retreat is therefore not simply a period of sitting quietly. It is a structured opportunity to integrate ethical conduct, mental cultivation, contemplation, and instruction.

### 13. A Progressive Path: From Ethical Discipline to Liberation

Although AN 4.170 describes four approaches to Arahantship, a practical training program must also consider how practitioners prepare for sustained meditation.

For this reason, our training places the four approaches within the broader progression of the liberation path.

**Stage One: Establishing Ethical Discipline**

The practitioner begins by reducing conduct that reinforces worldly craving, aversion, and confusion.

Ethical discipline establishes a foundation of sense restraint and spiritual responsibility. It also helps reduce the remorse and agitation that can arise from harmful conduct.

For those undertaking more intensive renunciant training, discipline becomes progressively more comprehensive.

**Stage Two: Simplifying Life and Cultivating Renunciation**

The practitioner examines the habitual pursuit of sensory gratification, possessions, entertainment, recognition, and other worldly concerns.

The purpose is not to reject the world through hostility. It is to understand the role that worldly attachment plays in maintaining worldly bondage and to create the conditions for sustained spiritual cultivation.

A simpler life can provide more time and energy for meditation.

**Stage Three: Developing Samatha**

The practitioner establishes a regular meditation practice and develops the ability to remain with a chosen object.

Attention becomes more stable, distraction is increasingly recognized, and the mind develops the capacity for sustained concentration.

The degree of concentration required and the appropriate methods depend on the practitioner and the training undertaken.

**Stage Four: Developing Vipassanā**

The practitioner investigates the processes of experience and the movements of attraction, aversion, and ignorance.

The aim is to develop direct understanding of the conditions that sustain worldly bondage and to cultivate the vipassanā necessary for liberation from worldly bondage.

**Stage Five: Coordinating Samatha and Vipassanā**

As practice matures, samatha and vipassanā support one another.

The practitioner learns to cultivate concentration without becoming attached to it and to investigate experience without allowing investigation to degenerate into restlessness.

The training becomes increasingly integrated.

**Stage Six: Deepening Samādhi and the Inward Establishment of the Citta**

The practitioner continues to cultivate mental unification and inward stability.

For those following the fourth approach described in AN 4.170, particular importance is given to the transition from Dhamma-related agitation to the inward establishment of the citta into the Higher Self / Soul-Spirit.

**Stage Seven: The Arising and Cultivation of the Liberating Path**

The four approaches converge upon the central objective described by the sutta: the arising, development, and cultivation of the path through which the fetters are abandoned and the underlying tendencies eliminated.

This final objective cannot be reduced to a prescribed number of meditation sessions or a fixed period of retreat.

A retreat can establish conditions for practice, deepen understanding, and help a practitioner overcome obstacles. The completion of the path requires the actual realization of liberation.

The progression outlined here is a practical training framework developed from the sutta\'s principles. It should not be mistaken for a claim that AN 4.170 itself prescribes these seven stages in this exact order.

### 14. Why the Monastic Path Is Central to Arahanism

Arahanism places Arahantship at the centre of its spiritual objective.

The training is therefore oriented toward a fundamental reorganization of life around worldly renunciation, meditation, and liberation from worldly bondage.

In our understanding, the full monastic life provides the most comprehensive institutional and practical framework for undertaking this work. The discipline of a bhikkhu or bhikkhunī establishes a way of life in which ethical conduct, renunciation, sense restraint, study, meditation, and the cultivation of vipassanā can be integrated into a single commitment.

This does not mean that every person attending a meditation retreat must immediately undertake ordination. A retreat can serve as an introduction to the path, an opportunity to deepen an existing practice, or a setting in which a practitioner examines the possibility of a more comprehensive commitment.

Participants may arrive with different levels of experience and different capacities for renunciation. The appropriate starting point will vary.

Nevertheless, the direction of the training remains clear.

For those who aspire to the complete renunciant path, a retreat can be an important step toward understanding the discipline, responsibilities, and practical demands of monastic life.

For those already undertaking renunciant training, it can provide an opportunity to strengthen concentration, deepen vipassanā, and renew their commitment to liberation from worldly bondage.

Our aim is to cultivate a training environment in which the path to Arahantship can be studied seriously and practiced systematically.

### 15. The Ultimate Objective: Beyond Temporary Peace

Meditation can produce many benefits.

It can reduce agitation, improve concentration, support emotional balance, and help practitioners respond more wisely to difficult circumstances. These benefits are meaningful, but they do not exhaust the purpose of the liberation path.

Arahanism directs attention toward a more fundamental question: Can the underlying causes of worldly bondage be completely eradicated?

A person may learn to manage anger without eradicating the underlying tendency toward aversion. One may learn to control sensual desire without eliminating the deeper inclination toward attachment. One may develop profound concentration without overcoming the ignorance that maintains worldly bondage.

The training must therefore continue beyond the acquisition of useful psychological skills. Samatha and vipassanā needed to be developed to understand experience and the processes that maintain worldly attachment.

Samādhi establishes the mind in unification.

Sīla provides the ethical foundation and progressive withdrawal, while renunciation creates the conditions for sustained spiritual cultivation.

Together, these dimensions support the development of the path leading to Arahantship.

The goal is not to become dependent upon a particular meditative experience, nor to accumulate spiritual achievements while leaving the underlying causes of worldly bondage intact.

The goal is liberation from worldly bondage.

The Venerable Ānanda\'s teaching in AN 4.170 reminds us that the decisive work is the development of the path through which the fetters are abandoned and the underlying tendencies eliminated.

This is the standard toward which our training is directed.

### 16. Begin the Training

The path leading to Arahantship requires more than curiosity about spiritual matters. It requires sustained practice, ethical discipline, sense restraint, patience, and a willingness to examine the deepest habits through which the mind remains attached to worldly existence.

A meditation retreat offers an opportunity to begin or deepen this work within a structured environment.

Participants are introduced to the principles of samatha and vipassanā, learn to cultivate mental stability, investigate the movements of craving and aversion, and develop a clearer understanding of the relationship between meditation and liberation.

Those with previous meditation experience can use the retreat to strengthen their practice and examine obstacles that may have limited their progress. Those who are new to meditation can establish a foundation for continued training.

For practitioners considering the monastic path, the retreat also provides an opportunity to learn about the role of ethical discipline, simplicity, renunciation, and sustained meditation in the life of a renunciant.

Progress should be approached with patience and discernment. Unusual experiences, temporary happiness, and periods of concentration should not be mistaken for final realization. The practitioner must continue to cultivate the path and examine whether the underlying tendencies that sustain worldly bondage are genuinely being weakened and abandoned.

Our invitation is to approach the training with sincerity and a clear understanding of its purpose.

**The purpose of meditation is not merely to make life within the world more comfortable. It is to cultivate the path leading beyond worldly bondage.**

Through ethical discipline, sense restraint, samatha, vipassanā, we undertake the training directed toward the ultimate objective of Arahanism: Arahantship.

*The Arahanist Order of Canada offers meditation training for those who wish to understand and practice the liberation path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.*

### AN 4.170 Yuganaddha Sutta: In Conjunction - The Four Paths Leading to Arahantship 

Thus have I heard (*Evaṁ me sutaṁ*): On one occasion, the Venerable (*Āyasmā*) Ānanda was dwelling at Kosambī in Ghosita's monastery (*Ghositārāme*). There, Venerable Ānanda addressed the bhikkhus: \"Friends, bhikkhus!\" (*\"Āvuso bhikkhave!\"*).

\"Friend!\" (*\"Āvuso!\"*), those bhikkhus replied to Venerable Ānanda. Venerable Ānanda said this:

\"Whoever, friends---whether a bhikkhu or a bhikkhunī---declares the attainment of Arahantship (*arahattappattiṁ*) in my presence, all of them do so through four paths (*catuhi maggehi*), or one among them. What four?

**1. Samatha Preceded by Vipassanā (*Samathapubbaṅgamaṁ Vipassanaṁ*)**

Here, friends, a bhikkhu develops **Vipassanā** preceded by **Samatha** (*Samatha-pubbaṅgamaṁ vipassanaṁ bhāveti*). As he develops **Vipassanā** preceded by **Samatha**, the path (*maggo*) is born in him. He pursues, develops, and cultivates that path. As he pursues, develops, and cultivates that path, his fetters (*saṁyojanāni*) are abandoned and his underlying tendencies (*anusayā*) are eliminated.

**2. Vipassanā Preceded by Samatha (*Vipassanāpubbaṅgamaṁ Samathaṁ*)**

Furthermore, friends, a bhikkhu develops **Samatha** preceded by **Vipassanā** (*vipassana-pubbaṅgamaṁ samathaṁ bhāveti*). As he develops **Samatha** preceded by **Vipassanā**, the path (*maggo*) is born in him. He pursues, develops, and cultivates that path. As he pursues, develops, and cultivates that path, his fetters (*saṁyojanāni*) are abandoned and his underlying tendencies (*anusayā*) are eliminated.

**3. Samatha and Vipassanā Yoked Together (*Samathavipassanaṁ Yuganaddhaṁ*)**

Furthermore, friends, a bhikkhu develops **Samatha** and **Vipassanā** yoked in conjunction (*samathavipassanaṁ yuganaddhaṁ bhāveti*). As he develops **Samatha** and **Vipassanā** yoked in conjunction, the path (*maggo*) is born in him. He pursues, develops, and cultivates that path. As he pursues, develops, and cultivates that path, his fetters (*saṁyojanāni*) are abandoned and his underlying tendencies (*anusayā*) are eliminated.

**4. Mind Agitated by Dhamma-Restlessness (*Dhammaddhaccavigghītaṁ Mānasaṁ*)**

Furthermore, friends, a bhikkhu\'s mind (*mānas/mano*) is shaken/overcome (*Vigghīta*) by turbulence/agitation (*Uddhacca*) concerning the Dhamma (*dhamma-uddhacca-vigghītaṁ mānasaṁ hoti*). There comes a time, however, when his *citta* fully establishes itself (*santiṭṭhati*) in High-Self (***Ajjhatta***) precisely (eva) (*ajjhattameva cittaṁ santiṭṭhati*), rests, becomes unified, and enters Samādhi (*ekodi hoti samādhiyati*). Then the path (*maggo*) is born in him. He pursues, develops, and cultivates that path. As he pursues, develops, and cultivates that path, his fetters (*saṁyojanāni*) are abandoned and his underlying tendencies (*anusayā*) are eliminated.

**Conclusion**

Whoever, friends---whether a bhikkhu or a bhikkhunī---declares the attainment of Arahantship (*arahattappattiṁ*) in my presence, all of them do so through these four paths, or through one among them.\"

Commentary: On the Fourth Path

**1. *Ajjhattameva* =** Ajjhatta + eva

- **Ajjhatta** (*adv. / adj.*): Derived from Sanskrit **adhyātma** (adhi- + ātman).

  - Adhi- = \"high\" / \"higher\" / \"over\" / \"Superior\".

  - -atta (attā / ātman) = \"the self\" / \"soul\" / "soul-spirit".

  - **Meaning:** *higher self*, *soul*, *soul-spirit*, *Internal (higher dimension or the inner domain)* (in direct opposition to *bahiddhā*, external worldly).

- **Eva** (*emphatic particle*): *Only, precisely, strictly, indeed.*

**2. *Cittaṁ***

- **Cittaṁ** (*noun, neuter nominative singular*): Derived from \$\\sqrt{cit}\$ (*to perceive, think, be conscious*).

- **Meaning:** *Mind-heart, consciousness.*

**3. *Santiṭṭhati***

Santiṭṭhati = Saṁ- + ṭhā (tiṭṭhati)

- **Saṁ-** (*prefix*): *Together, completely, fully, thoroughly.*

- **ṭhā / tiṭṭhati** (*root verb*): *To stand, remain, stay, endure.*

- **Santiṭṭhati** (*3rd person singular, present active*): *Stands firm, settles down completely, becomes steady, establishes itself.*

**Structural & Textual Meaning**

**\"\...ajjhattameva cittaṁ santiṭṭhati\...\"**

*\"...the Citta settles down / stands firm precisely in Higher Self/ Soul-spirit precisely\...\"*

**The Dynamics of the Inward Orientation**

**Reversal of Movement:** Prior to this moment, the mind (*manas/mano*) was shaken/overcome (*Vigghīta*) by turbulence/agitation (*Uddhacca*) concerning the Dhamma.

**Inward Establishment (*Ajjhattameva*):** The awareness ceases projecting outward (*bahiddhā*) or grasping at surface mental phenomena (*anattā* aggregates). It turns 180 degrees inward to anchor directly in its Higher Self / Soul-Spirit (*adhi-attā*).

The \"higher self\" or "Soul-Spirit" is the **innermost, higher dimensional, and elevated core of being/existence**---as opposed to external, worldly attachments. It captures the essential truth of AN 4.170: that true breakthrough occurs when awareness ceases wandering outward to worldly existence and establishes itself firmly in the elevated depth, higher dimensional core essence within.

**Firm Standing (*Santiṭṭhati*):** The prefix **saṁ-** emphasizes completeness. The *citta* does not merely pause momentarily; it becomes thoroughly rooted and unshakeable in its inner center, it Soul-Spirit, opening the threshold for the Liberation Path (*maggo*) to be born.

Viewing the higher self or soul/soul-spirit through a **multidimensional structure** provides a clear framework for understanding sutta AN 4.170, why *adhi-attā* (the elevated/higher aspect of the self) functions as a deeply internal realization. When existence is understood across multiple dimensions of density, subtle energy, and awareness, the concept of a \"higher self\" describes the **higher-dimensional part of one\'s own being/existence** that operates beyond the dense, lower three-dimensional plane of physical and mental aggregates.

**1). Multidimensional Self vs. External World**

In many cultures, anything \"higher\" is projected outward into the sky as something external. However, in our multidimensional structural framework:

- **Lower Dimensions (3D / Dense Realm):** The physical body, surface sensory impressions, and the flickering, restless thoughts (*dhammaddhacca*) that react to external conditions (*bahiddhā*).

- **Higher Dimensions (Subtle / Transcendent Realms):** The higher-dimensional core of the spirit/soul (*attā*)---the quiet, luminous, and unshakeable core of human existence.

- **The Integration:** Because these dimensions exist simultaneously, accessing the higher-dimensional self is not an outward journey. It is an **inward descent/ascent** (*adhi-attā* -\> *ajjhatta*) into the deeper, higher-vibrational layers of one\'s own existing consciousness.

**2). *Adhi-attā* in AN 4.170 as Dimensional Shift**

This dimensional perspective illuminates the exact process described in the fourth category of **AN 4.170 (*Yuganaddha Sutta*)**:

1)  **Surface Restlessness (*Dhammaddhaccavigghītaṁ Mānasaṁ*):** The practitioner\'s mind is trapped in lower-dimensional processing---conceptualizing, analyzing, and wrestling with surface Dhamma concepts at the mental/intellectual level.

2)  **Turning Within (*Ajjhattameva*):** The practitioner shifts awareness away from lower 3D external inputs and intellectual chatter, turning directly into the inner, higher-dimensional axis (*adhi-attā*).

3)  **Unification (*Ekodi Hoti Samādhiyati*):** Through ***Samādhi,*** The scattered lower mind aligns and merges with the steady frequency of the higher-dimensional self.

4)  **Birth of the Path (*Maggo Sañjāyati*):** From this higher-dimensional alignment, true vipassanā and liberation from worldly bondage manifest, cutting through lower-dimensional fetters (*saṁyojanāni*).

**3). Guarding of the Higher Self/ Soul-Spirit (*Rakkhitatto*)**

This multidimensional understanding aligns with the imperative in early texts to **guard and protect the self/ Soul-Spirit** (*rakkhitatto*):

- The lower-dimensional aggregates (physical body, fleeting feelings, surface perceptions) are transient, world-bound, and non-self (*anattā*). They belong to the laws of cause, effect, and decay in this physical world (*loka*).

- The **higher-dimensional spirit/soul**, however, must be guarded (*rakkhitatto*) against becoming enticed by or entangled in those lower physical and mental manifestations.

- By disenchanting the lower, world-bound aggregates, the *citta* transcends the lower dimensions entirely, passing through the threshold of Brahma Pure Abode realm into the deathless realm of **Nibbāna**.

***Adhi-* as Both \"Higher\" and \"Inward\"**

If one views the *attā* (the core self/spirit) as something that must be protected, guarded (*rakkhitatto*), and purified from external defilements (*rāga, dosa, moha*), then turning the awareness **inward** (*adhi-attā* -\> *ajjhatta*) is literally elevating the mind away from low, worldly, external conditions toward its highest, uncorrupted state.

Understanding the self as a multidimensional continuum resolves any false conflict between \"internal\" and \"higher self\" (*ajjhatta* vs. *adhi-attā*). The higher self is not a separate external entity; it is the **higher-frequency, higher-dimensional anchor of our own spirit**, reached precisely by turning completely inward beyond the lower, dense surface mind.

In deep meditative practice, turning inward (*ajjhatta*) is not merely looking at biological organs or mundane thoughts; it is going deeper than the superficial, scattered, and restless surface mind (*dhammaddhacca*).

When the *citta* settles **internally to the Higher Self / Soul-Spirit** (*ajjhattameva*):

- It withdraws from external distraction (*bahiddhā*).

- It moves inward to a unified, quiet, and *samādhi* state (*ekodi hoti samādhiyati*).

- In modern spiritual language, going \"deep within\" to find that quiet center of *samādhi* and awareness is precisely what people mean when they say contacting or realizing their **\"higher self.\"**

**Bāhiya Sutta (Udāna 1.10)** is the most famous example in the entire Pāḷi Canon of the **4th path described in the Yuganaddha Sutta (AN 4.170)**---where a practitioner whose mind is stirred/agitated suddenly turns completely inward (*ajjhattameva*), aligns with the higher self / soul-spirit (*adhi-attā*), and attains immediate Arahantship. Here is how the Bāhiya Sutta connects directly to this dimensional breakthrough and liberation:

**1. The Agitation and Immediate Turning Within**

Bāhiya Dārucīriya was desperately seeking true liberation. After traveling across India without rest, he rushed up to the Buddha in Savatthī while the Buddha was walking for alms. Bāhiya repeatedly begged for instruction on the spot.

His mind was intensely urgent---a prime example of **dhammaddhacca** (spiritual urgency/restlessness regarding the Dhamma).

The Buddha gave him the concise instruction on sense-perception: *\"Diṭṭhe diṭṭhamattaṁ bhavissati, sute sutamattaṁ bhavissati, mute mutamattaṁ bhavissati, viññāte viññātamattaṁ bhavissati\...\"*

*\"In what is seen, there will be merely the seen; in the heard, merely the heard; in the sensed, merely the sensed; in the cognized, merely the cognized\...\"*

**2. Disentangling the Higher-Dimensional Spirit from Lower Aggregates**

The core of the Buddha\'s teaching to Bāhiya is the absolute separation between lower-dimensional, worldly phenomena and the true, guarded soul-spirit: *\"Tato tvaṁ, Bāhiya, na tena. Yato tvaṁ, Bāhiya, na tena, tato tvaṁ, Bāhiya, na tattha. Yato tvaṁ, Bāhiya, na tattha, tato tvaṁ, Bāhiya, nevīdha na huraṁ na ubhayantare. Esevanto dukkhassā'ti.\"*

*\"When, Bāhiya, for you there is merely the seen in the seen, merely the heard in the heard\... then, Bāhiya, **you are not \'by that\' (na tena)**. When you are not \'by that\' (na tena), **you are not \'in that\' (na tattha)**. When you are not \'in that\' (na tattha), then **you are neither here, in this current life/realm (na idha), nor next,** **in the next existence or hereafter (na huraṁ), nor in between the two (na ubhayantare)**. Just this is the end (anto) of dukkha.\"*

**The Dimensional Shift:**

- **The Worldly Aggregates (*Anattā*):** The sights, sounds, thoughts, and sensory processes belong to this physical world (*loka*). They are transient, subject to cause and effect, and are not our soul-spirit, nor part of our soul-spirit, nor belonging to our soul-spirit.

- **Not \'In That\' *(na tattha)*/ Not \'By That\' *(na tena)*:** Bāhiya is instructed to immediately stop identifying his true spirit (*attā*) with these lower-dimensional sensory inputs.

- **Neither Here, Nor Next (*Nevīdha na huraṁ*):** The purified *Citta* drops all spatial/dimensional location within the physical or mental universe. It is no longer bound \"here\" (in this current life/realm) or \"next\" (in the next existence or hereafter, next reincarnation).

**3. Immediate Realization of Arahantship**

Upon hearing this brief instruction, Bāhiya's mind was instantly freed from all taints right on the spot without remaining clinging (*anupādāya āsavehi cittaṁ vimucci*). He attained complete Arahantship in a moment.

Because he completely severed attachment to the lower-dimensional aggregates, when he was killed shortly after by a cow with a young calf, his final physical body dissolved.

The Buddha then uttered the solemn solemnity (*udāna*) regarding Bāhiya\'s ultimate transcendence: *\"Yattha āpo ca pathavī, tejo vāyo na gādhati\... Tadaññāāya munino, monena brāhmaṇo; Rūpā rūpā ca muccati, dukkhā muccati sabbaso.\"*

*\"Where water (āpo), earth (pathavī), fire (tejo), and air (vāyo) find no footing (na gādhati)\... Having fully known this (tadaññāya) for himself, the muni, the true Brāhmaṇa through pure inner awareness (monena); From form (rūpa) and the formless (arūpa) he is completely freed (muccati); from dukkha he is freed in every way (sabbaso)."*

***mona*** is **ñāṇa**: *higher direct knowledge*, **paññā:** *wisdom*, and **vijjā:** *transcendent vision*.

**Munino:** For the *muni* who has quieted the restless lower mind.

**Monena:** Through **pure inner awareness / silent sagehood**---not merely because he stopped talking, but because his *Citta* turned completely inward (*ajjhattameva*), disengaged from the four elements (*āpo, pathavī, tejo, vāyo*), and stood firm in its unconditioned core.

*Rūpā Arūpā Ca Muccati* **\"Freed from Form and the Formless\"**: The Arahant\'s *Citta* drops all clinging to both physical/material planes (*rūpa-loka*) and subtle non-material planes (*arūpa-loka*).

This verse highlights the final threshold of an Arahant - **Exiting the Universe**:

1.  **Transcending the Four Elements:** Physical manifestations and material aggregates (*anattā*) are built entirely of the four elements (*pathavī, āpo, tejo, vāyo*). When the Buddha states that these elements *find no footing* (*na gādhati*), it signifies that the unconditioned core of *Citta* has completely disengaged from material density.

2.  **Beyond Form and Formless:** By transcending both *rūpa* (dense 3D material worlds) and *arūpa* (subtle higher worlds), the Arahant is no longer bound to any realm within the law of death and rebirth (*saṁsāra*).

**Summary Connection**

  ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Aspect**           **Yuganaddha Sutta (AN 4.170)**                                                     **Bāhiya Sutta (Ud 1.10)**
  -------------------- ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Initial State**    Mind agitated/restless (*dhammaddhacca*).                                           Desperate, urgent search for the ultimate truth.

  **Pivotal Action**   Mind turns settled *precisely on the higher self / soul-spirit* (*ajjhattameva*).   Awareness immediately disengages from lower sensory aggregates (\"merely the seen in the seen\").

  **Transcendence**    Unifies (*ekodi hoti*) in *samādhi*, path is born, fetters abandoned.               Transcends \"here, next, and in between\"---complete liberation as an Arahant.

  **Final State**      Transcendence to **Nibbāna-Dhātu**.                                                 Freed from form (*rūpa*) and formless (*arūpa*), Transcending the 4 elements.
  ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

The Bāhiya Sutta is the ultimate real-world demonstration of collapsing the distance between lower sensory distraction and inner alignment with the higher self / soul-spirit (*ajjhatta*) to realize total liberation.

**Why This Instruction Leads Directly to Arahantship**

*\"In the seen, there shall be merely the seen; in the heard, there shall be merely the heard; in the sensed, there shall be merely the sensed; in the cognized, there shall be merely the cognized\...\"*

This brief formula is the absolute core mechanism of liberation. It strips away the entire psychological mechanism that creates ego-clinging and worldly bondage:

1.  **Severing the Conceptual Overlay:** Normally, when sensory contact occurs, the uninstructed mind immediately projects lower-dimensional narratives onto it: *\"I am seeing this, this is mine, I like this, I hate this.\"* That extra step creates the delusion of a localized ego interacting with external objects.

2.  **Stopping at Raw Perception:** By keeping perception strictly as *\"merely the seen\"* (*diṭṭhamattaṁ*), the process stops before identification or craving can ignite. The physical and mental aggregates (*rūpa, vedanā, saññā, saṅkhārā, viññāṇa*) are recognized as transient phenomena belonging strictly to this physical world (*loka*) and operating under cause and effect---they are non-self (*anattā*).

3.  **The Immediate Consequence (The Passage Following the Formula):** The Buddha immediately follows this by explaining what happens when you stop identifying with sensory aggregates:

*\"When for you there is merely the seen in the seen\... then **you are not \'by that\'** (na tena). When you are not \'by that\', **you are not \'in that\'** (na tattha). When you are not \'in that\', then **you are neither here, nor next, nor in between the two** (nevīdha na huraṁ na ubhayantare). Just this is the end of dukkha.\"*

**How This Relates to the Higher Self / Soul-Spirit (*Adhi-Attā*)**

When understood in a structural, multidimensional framework, this instruction is the precise key that frees the higher self (*adhi-attā*) from lower-dimensional entanglement:

\[ Lower 3D Realm / Aggregates (Anattā) \] \[ Inward Core/ Unconditioned (Adhi-Attā) \]

Sights, Sounds, Thoughts, Sensations \<===\|===\> Pure, Guarded Citta

(\"Merely the seen, merely the heard\") \| (\"Not \'in that\', not bound \'here or next\")

CUT HERE

- **Disentangling the Spirit from the Lower Aggregates:** The lower-dimensional aggregates (the 5 *khandhas*) are the raw sensory streams---the seen, heard, sensed, and cognized, sense-generated consciousness. They are not our Adhi-Attā, nor part of our Adhi-Attā, nor belonging to our Adhi-Attā.

- **Refusing Lower-Dimensional Identification:** As long as awareness believes *\"I am this body, I am this thought, I am this feeling,\"* the spirit/soul is trapped \"in that\" (*tattha*) dense, 3D lower plane.

- **Standing Firm in the Inner Axis (*Ajjhattameva*):** By reducing sensory inputs to *mere phenomena* (*mattaṁ*), awareness disengages from external objects (*bahiddhā*). The core awareness retreats completely inward into its own elevated, uncorrupted higher self/ soul-spirit (*adhi-attā*).

- **Complete Transcendence:** Once the *citta* is no longer \"in that\" (the lower sensory stream), it is freed from location \"here or next.\" It is guarded (*rakkhitatto*), isolated from lower-dimensional worldly defilements (*rāga, dosa, moha*), and completely ready to pass through the final threshold into the **Nibbāna-Dhātu**.

By cutting off lower-dimensional identification in a single moment, Bāhiya's awareness detached from all worldly aggregates, aligned instantly with its deepest internal axis (*ajjhattameva*), and achieved immediate Arahantship.

**Ajjhatta in Jain Tradition**

In Jain literature, both the term **adhyātma** (Prakrit: *ajjhattha*) and the title **Arhat / Arihant** (Prakrit: *Arahanta*) occupy a central position. Examining how Jainism treats these concepts reveals profound structural parallels---as well as important doctrinal differences.

**1. *Adhyātma* in Jain Literature: The Internal Science of the Soul**

In Jainism, ***adhyātma*** (*adhi* + *ātman*) literally means **\"pertaining to the ultimate inner Soul/Self (*jīva* or *ātman*)\"**. It forms an entire branch of Jain philosophy and mysticism known as **Adhyātma-śāstra** (the discipline of inward realization of Higher Self/Soul).

**A. The Core Meaning: Turning Inward Away from *Bahir-ātman***

Jain texts explicitly divide the human experience into three progressive stages of the self (most famously in Ācārya Kundakunda\'s *Samayasāra* and Śubhacandra\'s *Jñānārṇava*), division of the soul into **Bahir-ātman**, **Antar-ātman**, and **Param-ātman** is systematically laid out in Acharya Pujyapada's *Samādhitantra* (5th century CE) and elaborated in Acharya Yogindu's *Paramātmaprakāśa* (6th century CE) and Śubhacandra's *Jñānārṇava* (11th century CE).

**Acharya Pujyapada, *Samādhitantra* (Verse 4)**: Bahir-antaḥ paraś-ca iti tridhā-ātmā sarva-dehiṣu, Bahir-ātmā śarīrādau jātātma-bhrāntir, Āntaraḥ citta-doṣātma-vibhrāntiḥ, Paramātmeti kīrtyate.

In all embodied beings (*sarva-dehiṣu*), the self (*ātmā*) is threefold (*tridhā*): external (*bahir*), internal (*antaḥ*), and supreme (*paraś-ca iti*). The external self (*bahir-ātmā*) is the misconception of selfhood (*jātātma-bhrāntir*) regarding the physical body and external objects (*śarīrādau*). The internal self (*āntaraḥ*) is \[the state free from\] the delusion of selfhood (*ātma-vibhrāntiḥ*) regarding mental defilements (*citta-doṣa*); \[the third\] is proclaimed as the supreme self (*paramātmeti kīrtyate*).

**Acharya Pujyapada, *Samādhitantra* (Verse 7)**: *Bahir-ātmā samūḍhātmā, Kalevaram ahetukam swam eva abhimanyeta, Swa-para-avedakaḥ.*

The external self (*bahir-ātmā*) is a deeply deluded self (*samūḍhātmā*). Through illusion (*bhrāntyā*), lacking knowledge (*avedakaḥ*) of what belong to oneself (*swa*) and external/foreign (*para*), it falsely considers (*abhimanyeta*) the transient (*Ahetukam*) physical body (*kalevaram*) to be verily (*eva*) its own self (*swam*).

**Ācārya Kundakunda, Samayasāra (Gāthā 186)**: *aham edam udo cedi ya ajjhavasāṇaṁ karei jo jīvo \| so parabhāvaṁ ṇecchai ṇiyattade so a aṇṇāṇā \|\|*

Which soul (*jo jīvo*) makes the mental delusion (*ajjhavasāṇaṁ karei*) that \'I am this, this is mine\' (*aham edam udo cedi ya*)\... That soul does not desire foreign modifications (*so parabhāvaṁ ṇecchai*), and that one turns away (*so a ṇiyattade*) from ignorance (*aṇṇāṇā*).

**Ācārya Yogindu Deva, *Paramātmaprakāśa* (Gāthā 11):** *ṇimmala-ṇāṇa-sahāu jiu kamma-malehiṁ vimukku \| so paramappā ṇicchaeṇa jo samāhihiṁ ṭhiu \|\|*

The soul (jiu) whose inherent nature is spotless, pure direct knowledge (ṇimmala-ṇāṇa-sahāu), completely freed from karmic stains (kamma-malehiṁ vimukku)\... Which stands firm in Samādhi (jo samāhihiṁ ṭhiu), that one, in absolute reality (so ṇicchaeṇa), is the supreme ***ātma*** (paramappā).

**Ācārya Yogindu Deva, *Yogasāra* (Verse 1):** *jiu ṇimmalu ṇāṇa-sahāu jiu kamma-malehiṁ vimukku \| appi-chahi jo maṇu dharai so bohiya paramappā \|\|*

The soul (*jiu*) whose inherent nature is spotless, pure direct knowledge (*ṇimmalu ṇāṇa-sahāu jiu*), and completely freed from karmic stains (*kamma-malehiṁ vimukku*)\... Whosoever holds their mind (*jo maṇu dharai*) in desireless contemplation of that Self / Soul ***ātma*** (*appi-chahi*), that awakened one knows the supreme ***ātma*** (*so bohiya paramappā*).

**B. Major Jain Texts Dedicated to *Adhyātma***

**Ācārya Haribhadra Sūri, Yogabindu (Verse 31):** *adhyātmaṁ bhāvanā dhyānaṁ samatā vṛttisaṁkṣayaḥ \| mokṣeṇa yojanādyogo eṣa śreṣṭho yathottaram \|\|*

The Higher Self / Soul (*adhyātmaṁ*), worldly manifestation / becoming (*bhāvanā*), Jhana meditation (*dhyānaṁ*), equanimity (*samatā*), and the complete destruction of mental fluctuations (*vṛtti-saṁkṣayaḥ*)--- From joining (*yojanāt*) with liberation (*mokṣeṇa*), this (*eṣa*) is yoga (*yogaḥ*); each successive stage is superior (*śreṣṭho yathottaram*) to the preceding one.

- **Upādhyāya Yaśovijaya (17th century CE):** Authored the famous **Adhyātmasāra** (\"The Essence of Adhyātma\"), where *adhyātma* is defined as the internal purification of the mind (*manaḥśuddhi*) and direct experience (*anubhava*) of the pure self.

**Mahāpadhyāya Yaśovijaya Gaṇi, *Adhyātmasāra* (1.1--4):** *ātmānam adhikṛtyaiṣā pravṛttir adhyātmam ucyate \| manaḥśuddhyādikā sā ca jñeyā samyag-vimuktaye \|\| ragādimatamattyāgaḥ śuddhir mana ity ucyate \| sā caiva svātmano bhāvaḥ śuddha-saṁvittilakṣaṇaḥ \|\| cittair viśuddhair manaso virago, virāgataḥ syād anubhūtir ātmanaḥ \| tato bhavet kevala-bodha-lakṣmiḥ, sa caiva mokṣaḥ sa sukhaṁ tad eva \|\| anubhavasi yadātvam ātmānaṁ svatantraṁ, parama-pada-samīsthaṁ śuddha-bodha-svarūpam \| tada iha sakala-duḥkhaiḥ mucyase saṁsārāt, labhase ca nija-mūlaṁ śāśvataṁ brahma-dhāma \|\|*

This (*eṣā*) directed activity (*pravṛttir*) having taken the Soul / *ātmā* (*ātmānam*) as its supreme subject (*adhikṛtya*) is declared as the Higher Self / Soul (*adhyātmam ucyate*). And (*ca*) that (*sā*), having mind-purification as its primary quality (*manaḥ-śuddhy-ādikā*), must be understood (*jñeyā*) for complete, right liberation (*samyag-vimuktaye*).

The complete abandonment of the intoxicated conviction of attachment and related defilements (*rāgādi-mata-māt-tyāgaḥ*) is declared as the purification of the mind (*śuddhir manasa iti ucyate*).
