And that \[purification\] is verily (*sā caiva*) the intrinsic state of one\'s own Self / Soul (*svātmano bhāvaḥ*), defined by pure, direct consciousness (*śuddha-saṁvitti-lakṣaṇaḥ*).

Through fully purified minds (*cittair viśuddhair*), detachment of the mind (*manaso virāgo*) \[occurs\]; From detachment (*virāgataḥ*), the direct experience (*anubhūtir*) of the Soul (*ātmanaḥ*) arises (*syād*). From that (*tato*), the splendor (*lakṣmiḥ*) of omniscience knowledge (*kevala-bodha*) arises (*bhavet*); and that verily is liberation (*sa caiva mokṣaḥ*), that precisely is ultimate bliss (*sa sukhaṁ tad eva*).

When (*yadā*) you (*tvam*) directly experience (*anubhavasi*) the Soul (*ātmānaṁ*) as self-dependent (*svatantraṁ*), situated at the supreme state (*parama-pada-samīsthaṁ*), whose intrinsic form is pure direct knowledge (*śuddha-bodha-svarūpam*)\... Then (*tadā*), here (*iha*), from all sufferings (*sakala-duḥkhaiḥ*) and from *saṁsāra* (*saṁsārāt*) you are released (*mucyase*), and (*ca*) you attain (*labhase*) one\'s own primordial root (*nija-mūlaṁ*), the eternal/deathless (*śāśvataṁ*) *brahma-dhāma*.

- **Acharya Pujyapada & Yogindu Dev:** Works like *Samādhitantra* and *Yogasāra* frame *adhyātma* as the direct contemplation of the uncorrupted soul, severing the bond with karmic particles.

**Ācārya Pūjyapāda, *Samādhitantra* (Verse 14):** *ātmānam ātmany evābhyasya samyag-jñāna-mayena hi \| prāpnoty ātmani samyag-jñānaṁ karmāṇi kṣapayaty api \|\|*

Having repeatedly contemplated (*abhyasya*) the Soul (*ātmānam*) in the Soul (*ātmani*) precisely (*eva*) through \[a mind\] consisting of right direct knowledge (*samyag-jñāna-mayena*), indeed (*hi*)\... One attains (*prāpnoti*) right direct knowledge (*samyag-jñānaṁ*) in the Soul (*ātmani*), and also (*api*) exhausts / severs (*kṣapayati*) the karmic particles (*karmāṇi*).

**(Verse 26-27)** Pūjyapāda details how contemplating the soul as uncorrupted and distinct from body/karma acts as the cutting tool that severs the karmic bond.

*dehāntara-gatenāpi dehe \'smin nātmanā saha \| ekatvaṁ bhāvyate \'yuktāiḥ kṣīrodakavat ātmanaḥ \|\| pṛthag-bhāvaṁ tu sañcintya deho \'yaṁ nātmanā saha \| dhyāyed ātmānam evāgre karma-bandha-vinirmokṣāya \|\|*

But (*tu*) having deeply contemplated (*sañcintya*) the distinct nature (*pṛthag-bhāvaṁ*)---that this physical body (*deho \'yaṁ*) is not (*na*) together with (*saha*) the Soul (*ātmanā*)\... One should principally meditate (*dhyāyed*) upon the Soul (*ātmānam*) alone (*eva*), for the complete severance (*vinirmokṣāya*) from the bond of karmic particles (*karma-bandha*).

**Ācārya Yogindu Deva, *Yogasāra* (Verse 12):** *kamma-malehiṁ vimukku jiu ṇaü saṁsāre hoi \| appa-jhaṇeṇaṁ jo ṭhiu kammaṁ ḍahai so i \|\|*

The soul (*jiu*) completely freed (*vimukku*) from karmic stains (*kamma-malehiṁ*) is not (*ṇaü*) in *saṁsāra* (*saṁsāre hoi*). Which \[soul\] stands firm (*jo ṭhiu*) through meditation on the *ātman* (*appa-jhaṇeṇaṁ*), that one verily (*so i*) burns up / severs (*ḍahai*) karmic matter (*kammaṁ*).

**2. Does *Adhyātma* Lead to \"Arahantship\" for Jains?**

**Yes, directly.** In Jainism, establishing oneself in *adhyātma* (turning inward away from material aggregates) is the exact path that leads to becoming an **Arihant** (Pāḷi/Prakrit: *Arahanta*; Sanskrit: *Arhat*).

However, the Jain definition of an **Arihant / Arahant** has its own precise technical framework:

**A. The Definition of a Jain Arihant**

In Jainism, an **Arihant** (literally translated traditionally as *\"destroyer of the inner enemies\"*---namely *krodha* \[anger\], *māna* \[pride\], *māyā* \[deceit\], and *lobha* \[greed\]) is a living human being who has attained **Kevala Jñāna** (omniscience/infinite direct awareness/knowledge).

- In the sacred Jain **Ṇamōkāra Mantra**, the very first bow is offered to the Arihants:

Ṇamō Arihantāṇaṁ \"I bow to the Liberated Worthy Ones\"

- An Arihant has destroyed the four destructive karmas (*ghātiyā karmas*) through total internal absorption in *ātma*.

**Ācārya Kundakunda, *Niyamasāra* (Gāthā 159):** *jo jhādi paramappāṇiṁ ghādi-kamma-kkhayaṁ kareṁti so hodi \| kevali-aṇaṁta-ṇāṇī aṇaṁta-suha-daṁsaṇo jīvo \|\|*

Which soul (*jo jīvo*) meditates upon (*jhādi*) the supreme Soul/*ātman* (*paramappāṇiṁ*), that one performs (*kareṁti*) the complete destruction of the destructive karmas (*ghādi-kamma-kkhayaṁ*)\... That soul becomes (*so hodi*) an omniscient one (*kevali*), possessing infinite direct knowledge (*aṇaṁta-ṇāṇī*), infinite bliss, and infinite direct perception (*aṇaṁta-suha-daṁsaṇo*).

**B. The Two Stages of Final Liberation in Jainism**

While an Arihant is fully liberated from inner defilements, Jainism distinguishes between two final states:

1.  **Arihant / Kevalin (Embodied Liberation):** The Arahant remains in a physical body, teaching the path until their remaining non-destructive physical karmas (*aghātiyā karmas*) exhaust naturally.

2.  **Siddha (Disembodied / Ultimate Transcendence):** When the physical body dissolves at death, the Arahant sheds the final physical frame and ascends to the **Siddhashila** (the top of the universe) as a disembodied, pure, eternal soul (*Siddha*).

***Adhyātma* in the Ṛgveda**

**adhyātma** (*adhi* + *ātman*) **does not explicitly occur as a formalized technical term in the core Ṛgveda Samhitā text**.

***Ātman* in the Ṛgveda:** In the oldest strata of the Ṛgveda, the word *ātman* appears, but its primary meaning is **\"breath,\" \"spirit,\"** or the **\"animating principle/spirit for humans\"** of physical phenomena.

**Ṛgveda 1.115.1:** *sūryo ātmā jagatas tasthuṣaś ca*

*\"The Sun (sūryaḥ) is the **ātmā** of all that moves (jagataḥ) and stands still (tasthuṣaḥ).\"*

**The Evolution of *Adhyātma*:** The tripartite exegetical framework dividing reality into three analytical categories---**Adhibhūta** (*physical/elemental plane*), **Adhidaiva** (*cosmic/divine plane*), and **Adhyātma** (*internal/microcosmic plane*)---only fully crystallizes later in the **Brāhmaṇas** (e.g., *Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa*), the **Āraṇyakas**, the **Taittirīya Upaniṣad**, and prominently in the **Bhagavad Gītā**.

In Vedic hermeneutics (*Nirukta* of Yāska), commentators retrospectively read the *adhyātma* (*inward / spiritual*) sense into late Ṛgvedic hymns (such as the *Nāsadīya Sūkta* RV 10.129 or *Puruṣa Sūkta* RV 10.90), but as an explicit compound term, *adhyātma* belongs to the late-Vedic and Vedāntic period, not the earliest Ṛgvedic layer.

## The Arahanist Order of Canada

**A Canadian Theravāda Lineage**

The **Arahanist Order of Canada** is a Canadian Theravāda lineage dedicated to preserving and transmitting Arahanism---the Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.

Grounded in the Theravāda Vinaya as preserved in the Pāli Canon, the Order establishes its distinct institutional identity around the systematic cultivation of worldly renunciation, ethical discipline, sense restraint, meditation, liberating wisdom, and the pursuit of Arahantship.

Arahanism places the renunciant path at the centre of spiritual life. The Order seeks to establish a living monastic community in which the Dhamma can be studied, practised, and transmitted across generations of dedicated renunciants. Its work includes supporting the formation of Bhikkhus and Bhikkhunīs, cultivating disciplined contemplative communities, and establishing the conditions necessary for sincere practitioners to devote themselves to the complete liberation of the mind from worldly bondage.

The Order\'s institutional identity is distinctly Arahanist, while its monastic discipline is grounded in the Theravāda canonical tradition. As a Canadian Theravāda lineage, it seeks to establish a sustainable monastic institution that upholds the discipline of renunciation, draws upon established traditions of monastic training, and remains open to respectful dialogue with other Theravāda lineages.

The Order\'s purpose extends beyond religious affiliation, ritual ceremonies, or institutional expansion. It exists to sustain the living practice of the Liberation Path, cultivate a community devoted to renunciation, and transmit its training to future generations. At the heart of this undertaking lies the ultimate objective of the renunciant path: **Arahantship and the realization of the Deathless.**

### 01 --- Our Religious Purpose

The Arahanist Order exists to give Arahanism a continuing communal and monastic form.

We understand the renunciant life as a progressive undertaking directed towards Arahantship. Through worldly renunciation, ethical discipline, sense restraint, meditative cultivation, and liberating wisdom, practitioners work to overcome *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*---the fundamental defilements that perpetuate worldly bondage.

The Order therefore places spiritual training at the centre of its activities. Religious buildings, ceremonies, educational programs, and institutional structures have value insofar as they support the practice and transmission of the Dhamma.

Our guiding progression is:

**Sīla → Samādhi → Paññā → Nibbidā → Virāga → Vimutti**

Sīla provides the foundation for ethical withdrawal and spiritual cultivation. Samādhi develops the concentration and stability of mind necessary for elevating the mind and deeper investigation. Paññā brings liberating understanding, leading towards disenchantment with conditioned existence, the fading of worldly passion, and release from worldly bondage.

Within this framework, ethical discipline of **Sīla** develops progressively through the Five Precepts, the Eight **Uposatha** Precepts, the Eight Precepts ordination of **Upāsaka / Upāsikā**, the Ten Precepts of novice renunciation of **Sāmaṇera / Sāmaṇerī**, and the full disciplinary obligations of ordained monastics **Bhikkhu / Bhikkhunī** under the applicable Vinaya.

The Order\'s ultimate objective is **Arahantship** and the realization of **Nibbāna-dhātu**, understood within Arahanism as the Deathless realm beyond worldly bondage.

The Order does not measure its success solely through institutional growth. Its defining purpose is to preserve the conditions for serious spiritual training and the complete liberation of the mind from worldly bondage.

### 02 --- The Canonical Foundation and Renunciant Inheritance

The Arahanist Order of Canada follows the Theravāda Vinaya as preserved in the Pāli Canon. The Vinaya Piṭaka provides the canonical foundation for monastic discipline, while the Sutta Piṭaka form part of the broader scriptural inheritance through which the Dhamma is studied and understood. The order does not use Abhidhamma Piṭaka as guideline for spiritual cultivation.

The Order recognizes that the Vinaya is integral to the renunciant life. It governs monastic conduct and the formal procedures of the Saṅgha, establishing the framework within which ordained practitioners undertake their responsibilities.

At the same time, Arahanism has its own distinct institutional identity and spiritual emphasis. Its purpose is to preserve and cultivate what it understands as Gautama\'s original Dhamma of Liberation Path, with particular attention to worldly renunciation, sense restraint and the systematic purification of the mind, and the attainment of Arahantship.

The Order therefore combines fidelity to its stated canonical foundation with a commitment to examine inherited interpretations and practices critically. It seeks to distinguish the essential requirements of monastic discipline from local customs, later institutional developments, and practices that may not directly serve the path of liberation from worldly bondage.

Renunciation, in the Arahanist understanding, involves more than outward religious observance. It requires a progressive withdrawal from worldly entanglements and a fundamental reorientation of life towards simplicity, voluntary poverty, sense restraint, contemplation, and freedom from worldly attachment.

The Order seeks to sustain an environment in which this commitment can be lived seriously. Scriptural study, monastic discipline, sense restraint, meditation, communal support, and spiritual guidance must work together in service of liberation from worldly bondage.

**An Independent Canadian Institution**

The Arahanist Order is establishing its own institutional identity in Canada. Although grounded in the Theravāda Vinaya and informed by established monastic training traditions, it does not claim administrative affiliation with, or recognition by, every national Saṅgha administration or Theravāda lineage.

The Order recognizes the importance of established traditions and seeks to maintain respectful relationships with the wider Theravāda monastic world. It aims to build these relationships through careful study, responsible practice, mutual understanding, and dialogue.

Its institutional independence does not exempt it from the applicable requirements of the Vinaya. Rather, it carries the responsibility to develop transparent standards, examine its ordination procedures carefully, and establish a disciplined community worthy of the spiritual purpose it serves.

The Order hopes that its continued commitment to canonical discipline and serious monastic training will contribute to greater understanding and recognition among other Theravāda communities.

### 03 --- Monastic Training and Brahmacariya

A central responsibility of the Arahanist Order is to support those who aspire to the renunciant life.

The Order seeks to develop systematic opportunities for monastic formation through which prospective Bhikkhus and Bhikkhunīs can prepare for ordination and undertake the responsibilities of monastic life. Training encompasses ethical discipline, sense restraint, the study of the Dhamma and Vinaya, the cultivation of Samādhi, the development of Paññā, and sustained contemplative practice.

Brahmacariya---the celibate and disciplined life of renunciation---occupies a central place in this undertaking. By relinquishing household responsibilities, sexual relationships, and worldly ambitions, the renunciant dedicates life to the cultivation of the path towards liberation from worldly bondage.

The Order recognizes the importance of preparation before full ordination. Practitioners may begin with the Five Precepts and deepen their commitment through the Eight Uposatha Precepts. Those who aspire to a sustained renunciant life may enter a more intensive period of training as Upāsaka or Upāsikā, wearing white yogi clothing and undertaking a disciplined daily practice.

Where appropriate, this may be followed by novice ordination as a Sāmaṇera or Sāmaṇerī, and subsequently by consideration for full ordination as a Bhikkhu or Bhikkhunī.

The Order envisages a staged formation pathway, with substantial periods of preparation and supervised training at each stage. Its ordinary institutional expectations include approximately one to two years of committed Upāsaka or Upāsikā training, followed by at least two to three years of novice training before consideration for full ordination. These are formation expectations rather than universal canonical minimums, and progression depends upon readiness, conduct, understanding, eligibility, and the applicable Vinaya requirements.

Following full ordination, monastic training continues. Newly ordained monastics require appropriate guidance and support under the relevant Vinaya provisions, including the traditional framework of dependence upon a qualified teacher.

Through this progression, the Order seeks to prepare practitioners not merely to receive an ordination, but to undertake the responsibilities of a disciplined renunciant life.

The long-term vision is to establish a dedicated monastic training centre where resident monastics can sustain their practice, prospective renunciants can receive systematic instruction, and retreat participants can experience an environment ordered towards contemplation and spiritual cultivation.

### 04 --- Local Arahanist Viharas: Small Communities, Living Practice

The growth of the Arahanist Order need not begin with large monasteries or extensive institutional infrastructure. It can begin with small local Arahanist viharas: communities organized around meditation, ethical discipline, sense restraint, spiritual companionship, and support for renunciant practice.

An Arahanist vihara may serve as a place for Dhamma instruction, meditation, observance days, retreats, and communal activities. Where suitable facilities are available, it may also provide accommodation for visiting practitioners and support more sustained periods of contemplative training.

Each vihara should reflect the same fundamental orientation towards the Liberation Path while responding appropriately to the circumstances of its local community.

These communities are intended to sustain regular practice rather than function merely as gathering places for occasional religious activities. Their daily and weekly programs should support ethical conduct, meditation, scriptural study, and the gradual development of renunciant commitment.

Small communities can establish the foundations of a wider religious network through responsible leadership, mutual assistance, consistent practice, and the transmission of the Dhamma.

As the Order develops, local viharas may form connections across Ontario and other parts of Canada, creating a network of communities united by their commitment to renunciation, spiritual discipline, and Arahantship.

The long-term objective is not uniformity in every local arrangement, but fidelity to a shared spiritual purpose.

### 05 --- A Community of Mutual Support

The renunciant path requires discipline and individual commitment, but a functioning religious community also depends upon generosity, cooperation, and care.

The Order seeks to cultivate relationships among monastics, lay practitioners, volunteers, and supporters grounded in mutual respect and responsibility.

Community members may support one another through Dhamma study, volunteer service, the provision of food and practical necessities, hospitality, transportation, and the maintenance of facilities for meditation and retreat.

Lay supporters help sustain the material conditions of monastic life, while monastics contribute through Dhamma instruction, spiritual guidance, the observance of discipline, and the example of a life dedicated to renunciation.

Such support is not an end in itself. It establishes the conditions in which the Dhamma can be practised and transmitted.

The Order aspires to cultivate a spiritual community that extends beyond differences of ethnicity, cultural background, language, and social status. Its communities should welcome sincere practitioners and visitors while maintaining appropriate standards of conduct, clear boundaries, and respect for the purpose of each vihara.

Mutual support must remain consistent with the discipline of renunciation. Community activities, administrative responsibilities, and material arrangements should enable monastics to maintain their practice rather than draw them unnecessarily into worldly preoccupations.

Through generosity, compassion, sense restraint, and shared commitment, the community can become a stable source of support for those pursuing the Liberation Path.

### 06 --- Serving the Wider Community

The Arahanist Order seeks to engage constructively with Canadian society through Dhamma education, meditation programs, retreats, and appropriate community activities.

These activities can introduce interested members of the public to ethical discipline, contemplative practice, and the value of a simpler and more reflective way of life.

Meditation instruction may help participants develop mindfulness, concentration, emotional balance, patience, and a more considerate response to life\'s difficulties. Dhamma education can also introduce them to the place of these practices within the broader framework of worldly renunciation and liberation from worldly bondage.

The Order\'s public engagement, however, remains connected to its distinctive religious purpose. Meditation is not treated merely as a relaxation technique, nor is the renunciant life reduced to general wellbeing. These activities provide opportunities to encounter the Dhamma and, for those who wish to proceed further, to explore the progressive training of Sīla, Samādhi, and Paññā.

The Order seeks to make its teachings accessible without obscuring their spiritual objective. Public meditation programs may serve people with different levels of experience, while more intensive retreats and monastic training are intended for those prepared to undertake deeper discipline and sustained practice.

The Order does not seek to establish itself through political power or cultural confrontation. It seeks to contribute through disciplined conduct, compassionate service, responsible community life, and the transmission of a contemplative tradition.

Its public activities should reflect the same principles that govern its monastic life: simplicity, ethical responsibility, sincerity, sense restraint, and a commitment to the Dhamma.

### 07 --- A Disciplined and Responsible Religious Structure

The Arahanist Order seeks to develop an organizational structure that supports its religious mission while allowing local communities to grow responsibly.

As the community develops, its structure may include a central governing body responsible for institutional direction and coordination, regional arrangements supporting local administration, and individual viharas serving practitioners in their respective communities.

Spiritual leadership and administrative responsibility should remain clearly defined. Presiding monastics provide religious guidance and oversee spiritual training within the scope of their responsibilities. Designated stewards and other appointed officers may assist with facilities, hospitality, resident arrangements, finances, and day-to-day operations, consistent with the applicable discipline and the Order\'s governance arrangements.

The Order also seeks to establish appropriate procedures for leadership, membership, community standards, and the development of new viharas. These arrangements should support accountability, continuity, and the integrity of the religious mission.

Institutional independence entails a corresponding responsibility to maintain transparent procedures and clear lines of accountability. The Order\'s standards should be understandable to its members, and its decisions should remain directed towards its stated religious purpose.

The organizational structure is intended to serve the Dhamma---not to become an independent objective or a substitute for spiritual accomplishment.

As the Order grows, its institutions should continue to be evaluated according to whether they support renunciation, facilitate serious training, preserve monastic discipline, and help sustain the conditions for liberation from worldly bondage.

### 08 --- A Vision for the Future

The long-term vision of the Arahanist Order of Canada is to establish a sustainable religious community centred on worldly renunciation, sense restraint, contemplative training, and the pursuit of Arahantship.

This vision includes the development of local viharas, a dedicated monastic training centre, facilities for residential meditation and retreats, a community of lay supporters, and educational resources through which Arahanism can be transmitted to future generations.

The Order seeks to establish conditions in which aspiring renunciants can prepare for monastic life, ordained monastics can sustain disciplined practice, and lay practitioners can deepen their understanding of the Dhamma while supporting the conditions necessary for renunciation.

It also envisions a community in which women and men who sincerely aspire to the renunciant life can undertake serious spiritual training. The Order is committed to developing a path of formation for both Bhikkhus and Bhikkhunīs, while recognizing that the external recognition associated with Bhikkhunī ordination remain subjects of disagreement among Theravāda institutions.

The Order intends to address these matters through careful study, transparent institutional standards, and respectful engagement with qualified monastics and other Theravāda communities.

The development of this community will require time, resources, capable leadership, and sustained commitment from practitioners and supporters. The Order therefore regards the establishment of small, stable, and practice-oriented communities as an important foundation for future growth.

The success of the Order will not be measured solely by the number of its members, the size of its properties, or the extent of its institutional network. These may support its development, but its defining purpose remains the preservation and practice of the Liberation Path.

The vision is to establish a living Canadian monastic institution capable of sustaining renunciant training, transmitting the Dhamma, and supporting future generations in their pursuit of liberation from worldly bondage.

### 09 --- Our Commitment

The Arahanist Order of Canada is committed to the following principles:

- **Preserving the Dhamma:** Transmitting the original Liberation Path as understood by Arahanism, grounded in the Theravāda canonical tradition and directed towards Arahantship.

- **Maintaining monastic discipline:** Following the Theravāda Vinaya as the canonical foundation of monastic conduct and the formal acts of the Saṅgha.

- **Supporting renunciation:** Cultivating the conditions for dedicated monastic training, Brahmacariya, sense restraint, and the progressive relinquishment of worldly attachments.

- **Developing contemplative communities:** Establishing viharas and retreat environments centred on Sīla, Samādhi, Paññā, and sustained meditation.

- **Supporting women and men on the renunciant path:** Developing opportunities for serious monastic formation and Bhikkhu and Bhikkhunī ordination.

- **Cultivating mutual support:** Encouraging generosity, responsible service, compassion, and cooperation among monastics and lay practitioners.

- **Serving through the Dhamma:** Providing appropriate opportunities for public education, meditation, retreats, and engagement with the wider community.

- **Maintaining institutional responsibility:** Developing transparent governance, clear standards, and responsible procedures consistent with the Order\'s religious mission.

- **Engaging with other Theravāda traditions:** Pursuing mutual understanding and respectful dialogue with other Theravāda traditions and religious institutions.

- **Transmitting the path:** Building a sustainable religious community capable of supporting future generations of practitioners.

**The Purpose of the Order**

The Arahanist Order of Canada exists to give enduring communal form to Arahanism---the Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.

Its work begins with the discipline of individual practitioners, develops through communities committed to worldly renunciation and mutual support, and extends towards the establishment of a living Canadian monastic tradition.

The Order seeks to preserve the Theravāda canonical foundation of its monastic discipline while cultivating its distinct Arahanist identity and spiritual purpose. Through worldly renunciation, ethical conduct, sense restraint, samatha, vipassanā, and liberating wisdom, it seeks to establish the conditions in which practitioners can devote their lives to the complete liberation of the mind from worldly bondage.

Its institutional development is a means to this end. Its viharas, training programs, monastic formation, public activities, and community structures all derive their purpose from the path they are intended to sustain.

**Our purpose is to preserve the Liberation Path, support the training of renunciants, and sustain a community dedicated to Arahantship and the realization of the Deathless.**

### 10 --- Our Mission

**Preserving and Transmitting Gautama's Liberation Path**

**Arahanism --- The Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.**

The **Arahanist Order of Canada** is dedicated to upholding and transmitting Arahanism---the Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant---within the Theravāda tradition. Its supreme spiritual purpose is the restoration of renunciation and Arahantship as the fulfilment of the religious life: the complete liberation of citta from worldly bondage termed as rāga, dosa, and moha, culminating in the realization of Nibbāna-dhātu, the deathless realm.

To fulfil this purpose, the Order is committed to preserving Gautama\'s original liberation teaching and the Pāḷi Canon, training renunciants in the complete path to Arahantship, teaching and publishing the Dhamma, sharing knowledge openly for the benefit of humanity, and establishing sustainable monastic communities in Canada. Through these interconnected responsibilities, the Order seeks to preserve a living Theravāda lineage in which the Dhamma is studied, practised, and transmitted for the welfare of future generations.

#### 1. Restoring Renunciation and Arahantship as the Supreme Spiritual Purpose

Worldly renunciation is the foundation of Gautama\'s Liberation Path. It is not merely a change of lifestyle, an outward expression of religious devotion, or a temporary withdrawal from ordinary responsibilities. It is a progressive relinquishment of worldly attachments and the conditions that bind citta to worldly existence.

Arahanism holds that the ultimate purpose of spiritual life is complete liberation from worldly bondage through the eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha. These three fundamental defilements sustain worldly yoke: rāga encompasses worldly craving, passion, and attachment; dosa encompasses worldly survival instincts of fear, aversion, anger, hatred and hostility; and moha encompasses ignorance and delusion concerning the nature of worldly existence and the path to liberation from worldly bondage.

The renunciant undertaking is directed towards the complete overcoming of these defilements of the mind-heart *citta*. Through the progressive cultivation of Sīla, Samādhi, and Paññā, practitioners develop ethical withdrawal, sense restraint, inner purification, meditative concentration, and liberating wisdom. This training leads towards Nibbidā, disenchantment with worldly conditioned existence; Virāga, the fading of worldly passion and attachment; and ultimately Vimutti, liberation from worldly bondage.

Arahanism therefore places Arahantship at the centre of its spiritual purpose. Arahantship represents the fulfilment of the Liberation Path through the complete eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha. It is not simply an elevated spiritual attainment or an ideal to be contemplated, but the supreme objective towards which the full renunciant life is directed.

For those undertaking the complete renunciant vocation, this means dedicating their lives to Brahmacariya, monastic discipline, celibacy, sense restraint, meditation, scriptural study, and the progressive relinquishment of worldly attachments. Spiritual training must remain directed towards the purification of citta and the realization of Nibbāna-dhātu, the Deathless realm beyond the universe.

The Arahanist Order of Canada seeks to establish the communal and institutional conditions necessary for this undertaking. Through progressive lay preparation, novice training, and full monastic ordination, it aims to support practitioners who sincerely aspire to dedicate their lives to the Liberation Path.

Worldly renunciation is therefore not simply the outward abandonment of worldly life. Its ultimate purpose is the complete liberation of citta from the bonds that perpetuate worldly existence.

#### 2. Upholding Gautama\'s Original Liberation Teaching

Arahanism is dedicated to upholding the original liberation teaching of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, the Sammāsambuddha. Its doctrinal foundation is the understanding that the ultimate purpose of spiritual cultivation is not the improvement, refinement, or continuation of worldly existence, but liberation from worldly bondage and the realization of Nibbāna-dhātu.

We understand Gautama\'s original teaching as a complete path of spiritual cultivation in which Sīla, Samādhi, and Paññā work together towards the purification of citta and the eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha. The fulfilment of this training is expressed through the progression from Nibbidā to Virāga and ultimately Vimutti.

Arahanism maintains that the Liberation Path must be understood in its entirety, including its purpose, its renunciant orientation, its discipline, its contemplative development, and its final destination. Individual teachings and practices must therefore be examined in relation to their contribution to the complete liberation of the practitioner.

Grounded in the Theravāda canonical inheritance, Arahanism maintains its own distinct doctrinal and institutional identity. It draws upon the Pāḷi Canon as its primary textual foundation while undertaking a critical examination of inherited interpretations and subsequent religious developments.

This examination includes fundamental questions concerning worldly renunciation, the nature of worldly bondage, the relationship between citta and the world, the interpretation of attā and anattā, and the destination of the liberated individual. Arahanism maintains its own doctrinal positions on these matters and seeks to articulate them through careful textual study, philosophical examination, and contemplative practice.

Upholding Gautama\'s original teaching requires more than the preservation of religious terminology or the repetition of inherited explanations. It requires a sustained commitment to understanding the purpose and coherence of the Liberation Path and ensuring that its central objective is not obscured by interpretations or practices that direct attention towards worldly attainment.

Arahanism therefore seeks neither to establish a new path independent of Gautama nor merely to reproduce later institutional forms. Its aspiration is to recover, preserve, articulate, and transmit what it understands as Gautama\'s True Dhamma (satdhamma): the teaching leading towards complete liberation from worldly bondage.

Through the Arahanist Order of Canada, this doctrinal commitment is given practical expression through canonical study, monastic education, disciplined spiritual cultivation, and the establishment of a living renunciant tradition.

#### 3. Preserving the Pāḷi Canon and Theravāda Textual Heritage

The preservation and study of the Pāḷi Canon constitute an essential responsibility of the Arahanist Order of Canada. The canonical teachings provide the primary textual foundation through which we examine, understand, and transmit Gautama\'s Liberation Path.

We regard the preservation of the Theravāda textual inheritance as more than the safeguarding of historical manuscripts or religious literature. It is the preservation of a substantial body of teachings concerning ethical discipline, sense restraint, meditative cultivation, wisdom, renunciation, and liberation.

The Pāḷi Canon provides the primary textual foundation for Arahanism through the Sutta and Vinaya Piṭakas, which preserve Gautama\'s teachings and the discipline governing the renunciant life. Arahanism does not adopt the Abhidhamma Piṭaka as part of its authoritative doctrinal foundation, regarding it as a later development beyond the original body of teachings. Even within the Sutta Piṭaka, Arahanism applies careful textual, historical, and doctrinal discernment, examining the language, terminology, context, consistency, and practical purpose of individual teachings. Particular attention is given to suttas that appear to preserve earlier layers of Gautama\'s doctrine and contemplative practices, allowing the original framework of the liberation path towards Arahantship to be studied and understood with greater clarity.

Accordingly, Arahanism places particular importance on studying particular canonical sources in Pāḷi while making their contents accessible through translation, explanation, and systematic examination. Pāḷi terminology is retained wherever necessary to preserve doctrinal precision, especially when familiar translations may not adequately communicate the original meaning.

Our approach also involves critically examining inherited interpretations, commentarial explanations, and subsequent developments within the broader Buddhist tradition. Such examination is undertaken to distinguish canonical teachings from later interpretations and to investigate whether particular explanations remain consistent with the fundamental purpose of Gautama\'s Liberation Path.

We recognize that the transmission of religious texts across centuries involves complex questions of textual history, interpretation, and preservation. Therefore, careful scholarship, comparison of sources, and responsible translation are indispensable to our work.

The Arahanist Order seeks to contribute to this preservation through publications, translations, commentaries, textual research, and digital archival initiatives. By making our research and written materials accessible through scholarly repositories and open digital platforms, we also seek to support future generations of practitioners and researchers.

Our commitment is to preserve the textual foundations of the Liberation Path while ensuring that their meaning and practical significance remain available for serious study and spiritual cultivation.

The Pāḷi Canon is not preserved merely as an inheritance from the past. It is studied and transmitted as a foundation for understanding and undertaking the path towards liberation from worldly bondage.

#### 4. Training Renunciants in the Complete Path to Arahantship

The Arahanist Order of Canada is dedicated to establishing a systematic framework for renunciant training directed towards the complete liberation of citta from worldly bondage and the attainment of Arahantship.

We regard renunciation as a progressive undertaking that requires more than formal religious affiliation or ordination. It involves a gradual transformation of one\'s way of life through ethical discipline, sense restraint, celibacy, meditation, wisdom, and the relinquishment of worldly attachments.

Accordingly, the Order seeks to provide progressive stages of preparation and training through which sincere practitioners may undertake increasingly complete forms of renunciation.

**Progressive Preparation for Renunciant Life**

For lay practitioners, the Five Precepts provide the initial ethical foundation. Those wishing to undertake more substantial renunciant discipline may observe the Eight Precepts on Uposatha days, cultivating temporary restraint, simplicity, and greater freedom from ordinary worldly entanglement.

The Order also provides a framework for resident lay practitioners undertaking a more committed religious life as Upāsakas and Upāsikās. Through guidance, ethical discipline, celibacy, and participation in the communal life of the Order, they may prepare themselves for formal novice ordination.

**Novice Ordination and Monastic Formation**

The next stage is Pabbajjā, the going forth into novice renunciant life as a Sāmaṇera or Sāmaṇerī, undertaken through the acceptance of the Ten Precepts.

Novice training introduces a more comprehensive form of renunciation, including monastic discipline, celibacy, simplicity, meditation, scriptural study, and the cultivation of proper conduct. It provides the foundation for developing the discipline and understanding necessary for full monastic ordination.

**Full Monastic Ordination**

The culmination of this progressive training is Upasampadā, full ordination as a Bhikkhu or Bhikkhunī under the Theravāda Vinaya and the disciplinary framework of the Pāṭimokkha.

Full monastic life represents a complete commitment to the renunciant vocation. It requires sustained dedication to ethical discipline, sense restraint, meditation, wisdom, communal responsibilities, and the continuous relinquishment of worldly attachments.

The Order recognizes the importance of preserving the traditional monastic standards and disciplinary requirements governing full ordination. It also seeks to establish appropriate conditions for the training and support of both male and female renunciants, including the future development of a Bhikkhunī training and ordination framework in accordance with the relevant Vinaya requirements.

**The Complete Path of Spiritual Cultivation**

Throughout these stages, the central objective remains the progressive cultivation of Sīla, Samādhi, and Paññā, leading towards Nibbidā, Virāga, and Vimutti.

Training is therefore not measured merely by the number of years spent in religious life, the acquisition of formal titles, or the completion of institutional requirements. Its deeper purpose is the transformation of citta through the progressive eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha.

The Order seeks to provide an environment conducive to this undertaking through monastic residence, meditation facilities, Dhamma instruction, canonical education, spiritual guidance, and a disciplined communal way of life.

Ultimately, the purpose of renunciant training is not simply to produce ordained members or maintain a religious institution. It is to cultivate practitioners who sincerely dedicate their lives to the complete Liberation Path and aspire towards the realization of Nibbāna-dhātu.

The Arahanist Order therefore regards the establishment of a living renunciant training tradition as one of its most important responsibilities.

#### 5. Teaching, Publishing, and Transmitting the Dhamma

The Arahanist Order of Canada regards the teaching and transmission of Gautama\'s Liberation Path as an essential responsibility. Preserving the true Dhamma requires not only maintaining its textual foundations and training renunciants, but also making its teachings accessible to those who wish to study, understand, and practise the path.

Through Dhamma instruction, meditation guidance, publications, translations, commentaries, and scholarly research, the Order seeks to communicate the principles and practical methods of liberation to both renunciants and interested lay practitioners.

**Dhamma Teaching and Spiritual Education**

The Order seeks to provide systematic Dhamma education grounded in the Pāḷi Canon and the Theravāda textual tradition. Its teaching encompasses ethical discipline, sense restraint, renunciation, Samādhi, Paññā, and the progressive cultivation of the complete Liberation Path.

Meditation instruction is an important component of this undertaking. Through appropriate guidance in Samatha and Vipassanā, practitioners are encouraged to develop concentration, contemplative understanding, and the wisdom necessary for overcoming worldly attachment.

The purpose of teaching is not merely to communicate religious knowledge or philosophical concepts, but to help practitioners understand the path and undertake its cultivation in their own lives.

**Publications, Translation, and Research**

The Order is committed to developing and publishing books, translations, commentaries, and research materials that contribute to the understanding and transmission of Gautama\'s liberation teaching.

These publications include studies of the Pāḷi Canon, comparative examinations of religious and philosophical traditions, explorations of renunciant spirituality, and systematic explanations of the principles underlying Arahanism.

We place particular importance on preserving original Pāḷi terminology while making the teachings accessible to contemporary readers through careful translation and explanation.

Through these efforts, we seek to contribute to scholarly discussion, support serious spiritual study, and provide reliable resources for future generations.

**Sharing Knowledge Freely for the Benefit of Humanity**

We believe that knowledge should be preserved and shared freely for the benefit of humanity. This commitment is especially important concerning the Dhamma, whose ultimate purpose is the liberation of beings from worldly bondage.

Accordingly, the Order extends its commitment to publication beyond conventional printed books and commercial distribution. We seek to make our written teachings, translations, commentaries, and research openly accessible through modern digital technologies.

As part of this initiative, the Arahanism Publications corpus is made available through two complementary international platforms: Zenodo, providing scholarly archival records with DOI identification, and Hugging Face, providing structured, machine-readable datasets for computational research and artificial intelligence applications.

By preparing our publications in accessible digital formats, we aim to facilitate their use by practitioners, scholars, researchers, developers, and future knowledge systems. This includes opportunities for digital preservation, computational textual analysis, searchable knowledge repositories, and AI-assisted research.

Our digital publications are made available under open licensing arrangements, allowing others to access, share, and build upon these resources in accordance with the applicable licence terms.

We regard this initiative as a contribution to the global knowledge commons. Our intention is not simply to distribute our own publications, but to make the knowledge developed through Arahanism available for independent examination, further research, and the benefit of future generations.

The technologies through which knowledge is transmitted will continue to evolve. Our responsibility is to make the true Dhamma accessible through these changing environments while preserving its original terminology, textual foundations, and liberating purpose.

Ultimately, teaching, publication, and research serve one fundamental objective: ensuring that Gautama\'s Liberation Path remains available to those who sincerely wish to understand and undertake it.

#### 6. Establishing and Sustaining a Living Renunciant Order

The Arahanist Order of Canada seeks to establish and sustain a living renunciant community dedicated to preserving and transmitting Gautama\'s Liberation Path.

We understand that a genuine renunciant tradition cannot be sustained through publications, doctrinal statements, or individual spiritual practice alone. It requires a living community of practitioners, experienced teachers, appropriate training environments, established disciplinary standards, and the conditions necessary for successive generations to undertake the renunciant life.

The establishment of the Arahanist Order is therefore directed towards creating a sustainable monastic and spiritual institution in which the true Dhamma can be studied, practised, taught, and transmitted.

**Establishing a Monastic and Spiritual Centre**

The Order seeks to establish a permanent institutional foundation in Canada, providing appropriate facilities for monastic residence, meditation, Dhamma education, communal chanting, spiritual instruction, and renunciant training.

Such a centre is intended to serve as more than a place for religious gatherings. It should provide an environment conducive to simplicity, solitude, discipline, contemplative cultivation, and the progressive relinquishment of worldly attachments.

The development of appropriate monastic facilities, meditation spaces, educational resources, and residential accommodations will form an important part of this undertaking.

**Cultivating a Living Renunciant Community**

The Order seeks to bring together bhikkhus, bhikkhunīs, novice renunciants, committed lay practitioners, and supportive members of the wider community in a shared commitment to the Dhamma.

The renunciant community provides the foundation for preserving monastic discipline, transmitting practical knowledge, supporting spiritual training, and maintaining continuity in the teaching tradition.

Lay practitioners also have an important role through material support, voluntary service, participation in Dhamma activities, and the cultivation of ethical and spiritual discipline. Their support helps establish the conditions in which renunciants can dedicate themselves to the full-time practice of the Liberation Path.

We therefore recognize the relationship between renunciants and lay supporters as an essential part of sustaining a living religious community.

**Preserving Monastic Discipline and Lineage**

The continuity of the Order depends upon maintaining proper standards of monastic conduct, ordination, training, and communal responsibilities.

The Order is committed to upholding the Theravāda Vinaya framework for full monastic ordination and discipline while developing its own institutional identity as a Canadian Theravāda lineage dedicated to Arahanism.

We also aspire to establish appropriate opportunities for both male and female renunciants to undertake the complete monastic vocation, including the development of a Bhikkhunī training and ordination framework in accordance with applicable Vinaya requirements.

Through the cultivation of qualified teachers, the training of successive generations of renunciants, and the preservation of established disciplinary standards, the Order seeks to ensure that its spiritual and institutional foundations can endure.

**Building for Future Generations**

The establishment of a living renunciant tradition is a long-term undertaking. It requires sustained commitment, responsible institutional stewardship, appropriate resources, and the cooperation of renunciants and lay supporters.

The Arahanist Order therefore seeks to develop its institutions progressively, ensuring that organizational growth remains directed towards its spiritual purpose rather than becoming an objective in itself.

Our aspiration is to establish a sustainable renunciant community in which the teachings of Gautama can continue to be studied, practised, and transmitted through successive generations.

Ultimately, the value of the Order lies not in the size of its institutions or the number of its members, but in its ability to preserve the Liberation Path, support genuine renunciant practice, and provide the conditions for the realization of Arahantship.

The Arahanist Order exists to sustain a living tradition dedicated to the Deathless.

### 11 --- Sharing the Dhamma with Humanity: Arahanism Publications Now Openly Archived for Scholarly Research and AI Accessibility

Summary

The Arahanism Publications corpus is now openly accessible through two complementary digital platforms: **Zenodo**, for permanent scholarly archival with DOI identification, and **Hugging Face**, for machine-readable distribution and accessibility within the open-source artificial intelligence ecosystem.

This dual-channel initiative represents an important milestone in our commitment to preserving, organizing, and transmitting the original liberation teaching of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, the Sammāsambuddha.

Beyond making our publications available to human readers, we have prepared their textual content in structured formats, including Markdown and JSONL, with UTF-8 encoding to preserve Pāḷi terminology and diacritical marks. This facilitates automated data processing, scholarly research, and potential integration into AI-assisted knowledge systems and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) applications.

Our motivation extends beyond digital publishing. We believe that knowledge should be preserved and shared freely for the benefit of humanity, particularly the Dhamma---the original teaching leading towards liberation from worldly bondage.

By making the Arahanism Publications corpus openly available under a Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International (CC BY 4.0) licence, we offer these resources to practitioners, scholars, researchers, developers, and future generations.

**This is our contribution to the global knowledge commons: preserving Gautama\'s liberation teaching and making it accessible to humanity through both traditional scholarship and emerging technologies.**

#### 1. A Milestone Worth Celebrating

The Arahanism Publications corpus has now been made openly accessible through two complementary digital archival platforms: **Zenodo** and **Hugging Face Datasets**.

This marks an important milestone in our ongoing effort to preserve, organize, and transmit the original liberation teaching of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, the Sammāsambuddha.

Through this dual-channel archival strategy, our publications are now available both for long-term scholarly preservation and for modern computational research and artificial intelligence applications.

**Zenodo** provides a permanent scholarly archival record, with DOI identification, allowing individual publications and research materials to be formally referenced and cited.

**Hugging Face Datasets** provides an openly accessible, machine-readable collection of our publications, structured to facilitate automated data ingestion, computational analysis, and integration into research and AI knowledge systems.

Together, these platforms establish two complementary pathways for the preservation and dissemination of Arahanism publications: one serving the enduring needs of scholarly documentation, and the other opening our written works to the developing possibilities of artificial intelligence and machine-assisted knowledge discovery.

This achievement is not simply about making books available online. It represents a commitment to ensuring that the dhamma teachings, research, translations, and writings developed through Arahanism can remain accessible to present and future generations.

We are pleased to mark this milestone and to share these resources with the wider human community.

#### 2. Knowledge Should Be Shared Freely

At the heart of this initiative lies a simple conviction: **knowledge should be preserved and shared freely for the benefit of humanity.**

This conviction is particularly important when it comes to the Dhamma---the original liberation teaching of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, the Sammāsambuddha.

The Dhamma is not merely a collection of philosophical ideas, historical records, or religious literature. It is a path of practice leading towards liberation from rāga, dosa, and moha---the underlying defilements that bind beings to the conditioned world.

Such a teaching deserves to be preserved, studied, understood, and transmitted across generations.

Our intention in making Arahanism publications openly accessible is therefore not limited to expanding the readership of our books. It is also to contribute to the wider body of human knowledge by making our research and written materials available to anyone who wishes to study, examine, reference, or build upon them.

We believe that the preservation of knowledge should not depend exclusively on commercial distribution or the limitations of physical publications. Modern digital technology offers opportunities to make written knowledge accessible on a much broader scale.

Accordingly, our digital corpus is made available under the **Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International (CC BY 4.0)** licence, allowing others to share and adapt the materials in accordance with its attribution requirements.

This reflects an aspiration that extends beyond our own institution: that knowledge may continue to circulate, that research may inspire further research, and that future generations may have access to the teachings and ideas preserved by those who came before them.

For us, making the Dhamma accessible is itself an offering.

May these publications serve as a resource for sincere practitioners, scholars, researchers, and all those interested in understanding Gautama\'s original liberation teaching.

May the knowledge preserved herein contribute to the welfare and benefit of humanity.

#### 3. Two Channels, One Purpose: Zenodo and Hugging Face

To achieve both long-term scholarly preservation and accessibility within the developing AI ecosystem, we have adopted a dual-channel archival strategy using two complementary platforms: **Zenodo and Hugging Face**.

Although both platforms make our publications openly accessible, they serve different purposes.

**Zenodo: Permanent Scholarly Archival**

Zenodo is an open-access research repository developed by CERN and supported through the European OpenAIRE infrastructure. It provides researchers, scholars, and institutions with a platform for preserving and sharing research outputs, publications, datasets, and other scholarly materials.

By depositing Arahanism publications on Zenodo, we establish formal scholarly records with Digital Object Identifiers (DOIs). These identifiers allow individual works to be cited, referenced, and located through stable academic records.

This provides an important foundation for the long-term preservation and scholarly recognition of our publications. Researchers and readers can identify specific works, reference them in their own research, and access the original materials without depending exclusively on commercial publishing platforms.

Zenodo therefore serves as our principal channel for scholarly documentation and archival preservation.

**Hugging Face: Open Access for Artificial Intelligence**

Hugging Face serves a different but equally important purpose.

It has become a major platform within the open-source artificial intelligence community, providing infrastructure for sharing datasets, machine-learning models, and related research resources.

By publishing the Arahanism Publications corpus as an openly accessible dataset on Hugging Face, we make our written works available in structured formats suitable for computational processing and automated data ingestion.

This creates opportunities for AI researchers, developers, and independent research communities to incorporate our publications into their own projects, including Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems, knowledge repositories, semantic search applications, and other AI-assisted research tools.

Rather than limiting access to conventional reading formats, we are making the underlying textual content available in formats that computers can process directly.

Hugging Face therefore serves as our principal channel for machine-readable distribution and AI research accessibility.

**One Purpose, Two Complementary Channels**

These two platforms represent different dimensions of the same commitment.

Zenodo helps ensure that our publications have identifiable and citable scholarly records. Hugging Face makes their textual content accessible to the wider computational research community.

Together, they establish an infrastructure through which Arahanism publications can be preserved, discovered, studied, referenced, and potentially incorporated into future research and knowledge systems.

Our objective is straightforward: to make these teachings and research materials accessible not only to readers today, but also to the technological and scholarly environments through which knowledge will be explored in the future.

#### 4. Making the Dhamma Accessible to AI

One distinctive aspect of this initiative is that our publications have not simply been uploaded as conventional digital documents. Their textual content has also been prepared in machine-readable formats to facilitate automated processing by modern artificial intelligence systems.

This distinction is important.

A conventional PDF or printed book is primarily designed for human reading. Although computers can extract text from many PDFs, the underlying structure may not always be convenient for automated processing, indexing, or retrieval.

Machine-readable formats provide a more direct way for computational systems to access and interpret written content.

**Markdown: Structured and Readable Text**

Our publications are made available in Markdown format, a lightweight text format that preserves essential document structures, including headings, paragraphs, lists, and other textual elements.

Markdown offers two important advantages: it remains easily readable by humans while also being straightforward for software applications to process.

This makes it particularly useful for organizing extensive collections of written teachings, translations, commentaries, and research materials.

**JSONL: Structured Data for Automated Processing**

We also use JSONL (JSON Lines), a format in which each line contains an independent JSON record.

This structure allows individual textual entries or document segments to be processed independently, making the corpus suitable for automated data pipelines, dataset loaders, indexing systems, and AI research applications.

For example, a developer building a Dhamma knowledge repository or a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) application can process the records and organize them for subsequent retrieval and reference.

Rather than requiring every document to be manually opened and processed, structured records make large collections of textual knowledge considerably easier to handle programmatically.

**UTF-8: Preserving Pāḷi and Its Original Terminology**

Particular attention has also been given to preserving Pāḷi terminology and diacritical marks.

Pāḷi contains characters that are essential to accurate transliteration and scholarly communication, including:

ā, ī, ū, ṅ, ñ, ṭ, ḍ, ṇ, ḷ, and other specialized characters.

The corpus uses UTF-8 text encoding, which supports these characters and allows them to be represented consistently across compatible software systems and digital platforms.

This is especially important for Arahanist and Buddhist textual research, where seemingly small differences in spelling and diacritics can distinguish different Pāḷi words and forms.

Preserving these characters helps maintain the textual integrity of our publications when they are processed, indexed, searched, or incorporated into other compatible digital research environments.

**Opening the Door to AI-Assisted Dhamma Research**

By combining structured Markdown documents, JSONL records, and UTF-8 encoding, we are making the Arahanism Publications corpus more accessible to modern computational research.

Researchers and developers can potentially use these materials to:

- Build searchable digital repositories of Arahanism publications.

- Develop Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) applications for accessing and referencing the teachings.

- Conduct computational analysis of Pāḷi terminology, textual relationships, and doctrinal concepts.

- Integrate the publications into AI-assisted research and educational tools.

- Develop specialized knowledge bases for studying Gautama\'s liberation teaching.

Importantly, making a dataset openly accessible does not automatically mean that every AI model will ingest it or incorporate it into its training. Actual utilization depends on the developers, researchers, and systems that choose to access and process the materials.

Nevertheless, by preparing the corpus for machine ingestion, we remove an important technical barrier and make these publications available to a much wider range of future applications.

We are entering an era in which artificial intelligence will increasingly participate in the discovery, organization, and transmission of human knowledge.

We believe that the original liberation teaching of Gautama should not be excluded from this developing knowledge environment.

By making our publications accessible in both human-readable and machine-readable forms, we hope to contribute to an environment in which the Dhamma can be studied, researched, and explored through the technologies of the present and future.

#### 5. Preserving Gautama\'s Liberation Teaching for Future Generations

The preservation of knowledge is important in every field of human inquiry. However, when it concerns the liberation teaching of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, the Sammāsambuddha, its significance extends beyond historical documentation and intellectual study.

The Dhamma is a path leading towards the complete liberation of the mind from worldly bondage subjectively termed as rāga, dosa, and moha. Its purpose is not merely to explain the nature of existence, but to guide those who undertake its practice towards Nibbāna-dhātu, the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

For this reason, preserving the authenticity and integrity of the teaching is essential.

Throughout history, religious teachings have been transmitted through different languages, cultural environments, philosophical interpretations, and institutional traditions. These developments have contributed to the diversity of religious literature and practice. At the same time, they make careful examination of the earliest available sources increasingly important for those seeking to understand Gautama\'s original teaching.

Our work through Arahanism is grounded in the study, preservation, and transmission of the liberation path as understood through the Pāḷi Canon and the Theravāda textual tradition.

We place particular importance on the original terminology of the Dhamma. Pāḷi words such as Sīla, Samādhi, Paññā, Nibbidā, Virāga, Vimutti, and Nibbāna carry meanings developed within a specific doctrinal and practical framework.

Translating these terms into modern languages is necessary for wider accessibility, but translation alone cannot always communicate their full significance. In some cases, familiar translations may introduce associations that differ from their original doctrinal context.

Preserving the Pāḷi terminology alongside translations, explanations, and commentaries therefore allows readers and researchers to examine the teachings more carefully.

It also provides future scholars with opportunities to revisit the original terminology, compare interpretations, investigate textual relationships, and develop a deeper understanding of the liberation path.

This is one reason why we consider digital preservation particularly important.

By making our publications available through permanent scholarly records and structured digital datasets, we hope to ensure that our research and interpretations remain accessible for independent examination. Readers need not simply accept our conclusions; they can consult the materials, investigate the sources, and engage with the teachings themselves.

We also recognize that artificial intelligence will increasingly influence how future generations discover and study religious and philosophical knowledge.

If authentic textual resources are made accessible to these developing systems, they may become useful materials for future research, comparison, and knowledge retrieval. Such accessibility does not guarantee accurate interpretation, but it creates an opportunity for more informed engagement with the original sources.

Our aspiration is that the teachings of Gautama will remain available for careful study rather than becoming increasingly distant from their original textual foundations.

Preserving the Dhamma is therefore not simply about keeping old books available. It is about preserving access to a path of liberation so that those who sincerely seek it may continue to investigate, understand, and practise it.

#### 6. An Offering to Future Generations

The completion of the Arahanism Publications digital corpus marks an important step in our ongoing commitment to preserving and transmitting Gautama\'s liberation teaching.

Through Zenodo and Hugging Face, we have established complementary channels for scholarly preservation and machine-readable accessibility. Through open licensing, we have made these resources available for others to study, share, reference, and build upon in accordance with the applicable terms.

Yet the significance of this undertaking is not measured merely by the number of publications archived, the size of the dataset, or the technologies through which it can be accessed.

Its deeper significance lies in the intention behind it.

We believe that knowledge should not be confined to one institution, one generation, or one medium of communication. What has been carefully studied, preserved, and understood should be made available so that others may continue the work of learning and discovery.

This is particularly true of the Dhamma.

The original liberation teaching of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant, the Sammāsambuddha, is a precious inheritance of humanity. Its value does not depend on the advancement of technology, the popularity of a particular religious institution, or the commercial success of its publications.

It is a teaching to be understood and practised.

Our aspiration is that these publications may reach sincere practitioners, bhikkhus and bhikkhunīs, scholars, researchers, students, and future generations who wish to investigate the original liberation path.

Some may encounter these teachings through a printed book. Others may discover them through a scholarly citation, a digital research repository, an online search, or an artificial intelligence application that draws upon openly available knowledge.

The means of access may change with time, but the importance of preserving the teaching remains.

We do not know how future generations will use these resources or what new technologies will emerge. What we can do today is make a sincere effort to preserve our work, organize it responsibly, and share it as openly as possible.

For us, this is an offering---not an offering intended to establish personal recognition, but one made in the hope that knowledge may continue to benefit others.

May these publications contribute to the preservation and understanding of Gautama\'s original liberation teaching.

May future generations have the opportunity to encounter the true Dhamma, investigate its meaning, and undertake the path towards liberation from worldly bondage.

May all who sincerely seek the Deathless discover the way leading to Nibbāna, the deathless realm.

**May the true Dhamma endure.**

**May the true Dhamma be preserved and transmitted for the welfare and benefit of humanity.**

*Sādhu! Sādhu! Sādhu!*

#### 7. Explore Arahanism Publications

We warmly invite practitioners, scholars, researchers, developers, and interested readers to explore the Arahanism Publications corpus and make use of these openly accessible resources.

Our publications are available through the following platforms:

**1. Zenodo --- Scholarly Archival Repository**

Explore the Arahanism scholarly archive on Zenodo, featuring permanent scholarly records and DOI identification for academic referencing, citation, and long-term preservation.

**Access the Repository:**\
<https://zenodo.org/communities/arahanism/>

**2. Hugging Face --- Open AI-Ready Dataset**

Explore and access the Arahanism Publications corpus on Hugging Face. The dataset is structured in machine-readable formats to facilitate automated processing, computational research, and integration into AI-assisted knowledge systems.

**Access the Dataset:**\
<https://huggingface.co/datasets/Bhante-Mudita/arahanism-corpus>

**3. Arahanism --- Official Website**

Visit our official website to learn more about Arahanism, its teachings, publications, research, and the activities of the Arahanist Order of Canada.

**Visit Arahanism:**\
<https://arahanism.org/>

**Our aspiration is to make the Dhamma accessible to everyone, everywhere, and across generations.**

May these resources contribute to the preservation, understanding, and transmission of Gautama\'s liberation teaching for the welfare and benefit of humanity.

*Sādhu! Sādhu! Sādhu!*

## About the Author

**Bhante Mudita Bhikkhu Thera**

*Founder and Spiritual Head of the Arahanist Order of Canada*

*Founding Ācariya of Arahanism --- The Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant*

Bhante Mudita Bhikkhu Thera is a Canadian Theravāda monk, meditation teacher, religious scholar, and author. His life and work are dedicated to preserving and transmitting Gautama\'s original liberation teaching, restoring renunciation and Arahantship to the centre of religious life, and establishing Arahanism as a living Theravāda lineage. Through monastic practice, doctrinal research, meditation instruction, publications, and the founding of the Arahanist Order of Canada, he seeks to make the complete Liberation Path accessible to those sincerely seeking freedom from worldly bondage.

#### 1. A Calling to Gautama\'s Liberation Teaching

Bhante Mudita Bhikkhu Thera is a Canadian Theravāda monk, meditation teacher, religious scholar, and author. He is the Founder and Spiritual Head of the Arahanist Order of Canada and the Founding Ācariya of Arahanism---the Liberation Path of Gautama, the Supreme Arahant. His wider religious and educational work also includes serving as Academic Director of the Buddhist College of Canada at Wutai Shan Buddhist Garden and Head of the Theravāda Division of the Buddhist Association of Canada.

His religious life is guided by a commitment to preserving, examining, and transmitting Gautama\'s liberation teaching, with particular emphasis on restoring renunciation and Arahantship to their central place in the religious life. He regards the complete eradication of rāga, dosa, and moha, culminating in liberation from worldly bondage, as the supreme purpose of the path taught by Gautama.

Through his monastic practice, canonical studies, meditation training, doctrinal research, and teaching, Bhante Mudita has undertaken the development of Arahanism as a systematic presentation of this Liberation Path. Rooted in the Theravāda monastic tradition, Arahanism seeks to preserve the teachings it identifies as belonging to Gautama\'s original dispensation, while critically examining subsequent interpretations and developments in religious doctrine and practice.

His work extends beyond the study and explanation of Buddhist teachings. It encompasses the translation and publication of religious literature, comparative studies of ancient spiritual traditions, the development of structured renunciant training, and the establishment of an institutional community dedicated to the cultivation and transmission of the Dhamma.

The founding of the Arahanist Order of Canada represents the institutional expression of this undertaking. Through the Order, Bhante Mudita seeks to establish a living Theravāda lineage in which canonical study, monastic discipline, sense restraint, meditation, renunciation, and the pursuit of Arahantship remain integrated within a coherent religious tradition.

At the heart of his work is a conviction that Gautama\'s Liberation Path must remain a living practice: studied carefully, cultivated through disciplined effort, and transmitted to future generations for the benefit of those who sincerely seek the Deathless.

#### 2. Monastic Formation and Spiritual Practice

Bhante Mudita\'s religious formation began with his ordination in 2013 at Pa-Auk Forest Monastery and Meditation Center in Myanmar, where he undertook intensive training within the Theravāda monastic tradition. His formation included the systematic cultivation of Samatha--Vipassanā meditation, study of the Pāḷi Canon, and observance of monastic discipline. This period established the foundations of his approach to the Dhamma, bringing together scriptural learning, ethical restraint, concentration, and contemplative practice.

Following this training, he spent a further five years practising within the Thai forest tradition. The simplicity and solitude of forest monastic life provided an environment for deepening renunciation, strengthening meditative discipline, and giving sustained attention to the practical dimensions of the path. This experience further shaped his understanding of the relationship between worldly relinquishment, mental cultivation, and the pursuit of liberation.

His years of monastic training in Myanmar and Thailand continue to inform his approach to religious education. He places particular importance on the integration of canonical understanding and direct contemplative cultivation. Scriptural study provides the framework for understanding the teaching, monastic discipline establishes the conditions for its practice, and meditation develops the concentration and wisdom necessary for progress along the path.

In November 2024, Bhante Mudita returned to Canada to dedicate himself to the systematic teaching and development of Arahanism. This marked a new phase of his religious work: bringing together his monastic formation, meditation experience, and canonical studies in the establishment of a structured teaching tradition and a developing renunciant community.

His teaching draws upon the disciplines cultivated through his years of practice, including ethical discipline, sense restraint, Samatha, Vipassanā, and the progressive development of wisdom. He emphasizes that the religious life requires sustained commitment to training and relinquishment, with Arahantship as its supreme objective.

The forest tradition remains an important influence on his understanding of renunciation, simplicity, and contemplative life. His present work seeks to carry these foundations into a Canadian institutional setting, creating opportunities for serious Dhamma study, meditation training, and the gradual development of a living monastic community.

#### 3. The Doctrinal Foundation of Arahanism

A central undertaking of Bhante Mudita\'s work is the examination and systematic presentation of Gautama\'s liberation teaching through Arahanism. His approach begins with the Pāḷi Canon, giving primary authority to the Sutta and Vinaya traditions as the foundations for understanding Gautama\'s teaching, the renunciant life, and the discipline leading towards Arahantship.

Within this framework, Arahanism distinguishes the early canonical foundations from subsequent doctrinal developments. In particular, it regards the Abhidhamma as a later systematization of Buddhist thought rather than an original foundation of Gautama\'s teaching, and therefore does not adopt it as an authoritative basis for its doctrinal framework. Commentarial literature and later interpretations are likewise examined critically, with attention to their historical development, textual foundations, and relationship to the teachings preserved in the Suttas and Vinaya.

Bhante Mudita\'s work seeks to examine the meaning, context, and practical purpose of individual teachings, giving particular attention to the relationship between renunciation, mental cultivation, wisdom, and final liberation. This includes reconsidering inherited interpretations of fundamental concepts and examining whether they remain consistent with the path and purpose presented in the early canonical teachings.
